- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-resilience::token_bucket` — `TokenBucket: Send + Sync + 'static` compile-time bound, multi-threaded-contention capacity-conservation proof (10 threads × 50 acquires against a 100-capacity zero-refill bucket sum to exactly 100), mutex-poison fail-open invariant (workspace `super::conn_rate_limiter` convention), zero-rate-with-default-burst one-token degraded-config pin, negative-rate clamp-to-zero-refill pin, NaN-rate / NaN-burst state-poisoning defence (`f64::NAN.max(1.0) == 1.0` and `f64::NAN.max(0.0) == 0.0` sanitize to a 1-token zero-refill bucket), long-idle refill saturation at `max_tokens` pin (60 s × 10/s would produce 600 tokens without the `.min(max_tokens)` cap), and sub-unit refill-rate fractional-token-accumulation pin (0.1/s takes 10 s to cross the 1.0 threshold and survives sub-threshold acquires)** — `crates/rustgate-resilience/src/token_bucket.rs` is the per-target rate limiter for outbound requests: each target endpoint is guarded by an independent `Arc<TokenBucket>`, tokens refill lazily on every `try_acquire()` call based on elapsed wall-clock time (no background task), and the `std::sync::Mutex<BucketState>` internal lock follows the documented workspace convention of failing-open on poison (so a panic elsewhere in the process cannot convert the rate limiter into a full outage of the guarded target). Before this PR the inline suite (6 tests) pinned happy-path burst allowance, custom `Some(burst)` override of the default `burst = rate_per_sec`, lazy refill after a real `tokio::time::sleep(200ms)`, the `.min(max_tokens)` cap during refill arithmetic, zero-rate + `Some(burst)` initial-burst-then-block behaviour, and the `.max(1.0)` floor on sub-unit burst inputs — but it left a material set of defensive invariants unpinned that a silent refactor could break. The one that matters most is **the capacity-conservation invariant under concurrent contention**: a rate limiter that double-grants tokens under a TOCTOU race is not a rate limiter; it's a false-positive layer that lets bursts of size `threads × burst` leak through under exactly the load profile a rate limiter is supposed to bound. The existing suite's time-based tests (`refills_over_time`) run a single-threaded happy path; a regression that replaced the `Mutex` guarding `BucketState` with a relaxed-ordering atomic + naive CAS (a common "optimization" path) could compile cleanly, pass every existing test, and over-grant by 10× under actual multi-core load. Eight new inline tests close the gaps. Trait-bound pin (one): `token_bucket_is_send_sync_static` uses a local `fn assert_bounds<T: Send + Sync + 'static>()` — a regression that added a `!Send` field (e.g. `Rc`, `*const _`) would fail this compile-time probe with a pointed error rather than at the far-away `Arc<TokenBucket>` boxing site in `rustgate-http::publisher`. Concurrency pin (one): `concurrent_acquires_do_not_exceed_capacity_under_contention` spins 10 threads each calling `try_acquire()` 50 times against a 100-capacity zero-refill bucket and asserts the total success count equals exactly 100 — never more (double-count / off-by-one overshoot) and never less (spurious reject); a refactor to a relaxed-ordering atomic without a proper CAS loop would trip this test. Fail-open pin (one): `mutex_poison_fails_open_not_closed` deliberately poisons the inner mutex by panicking a thread while it holds the lock, then asserts the next `try_acquire()` on a drained bucket returns `true` — the workspace convention (documented on `conn_rate_limiter`) is that mutex poison is an internal error that must not convert the rate limiter into a permanent drop; a regression that flipped the default to fail-closed would silently drop traffic after any unrelated panic in the same process. Degenerate-config pins (three): `zero_rate_with_default_burst_allows_exactly_one_request` pins the combined behaviour of the `burst = None` default (`burst.unwrap_or(rate_per_sec).max(1.0)`) and `rate_per_sec = 0.0` — the bucket must start with exactly one token and reject every subsequent request, because a regression that replaced the `.max(1.0)` floor with `.max(0.0)` would reject the very first request on a misconfigured deployment (silent fail-closed on a typoed config); `negative_rate_is_clamped_to_zero_refill` drives `rate_per_sec = -5.0` through a paused-time `tokio::time::advance(60s)` and asserts no refill occurs — a regression that used `.abs()` or dropped the clamp entirely would either flip the sign (spurious positive refill) or propagate negative tokens into the state and corrupt the `tokens >= 1.0` comparison; `nan_rate_and_burst_do_not_corrupt_bucket_state` feeds `f64::NAN` into both knobs — `f64::max` is documented to return the non-NaN argument when one side is NaN, so `NaN.max(0.0) == 0.0` and `NaN.max(1.0) == 1.0` together sanitize the construction into a 1-token zero-refill bucket; a regression that used `if rate.is_finite() { rate } else { 0.0 }` naively while forgetting the NaN case on the burst axis would let NaN into `BucketState.tokens`, where `tokens + elapsed * NaN = NaN` poisons every subsequent `>= 1.0` comparison and rejects every request forever. Refill-arithmetic pins (two): `refill_saturates_at_max_tokens_even_after_long_idle` is the time-domain sibling of the existing `does_not_exceed_max_tokens` test — where the existing test exercises a high refill rate in the single-call window, this one drains the bucket and advances paused time 60 s on a 10/s-capacity-10 bucket, then asserts only 10 acquires succeed before the cap kicks in; a naive `tokens += elapsed * rate` without the `.min(max_tokens)` post-clamp would grant a 600-token burst to the first caller after a period of idle, giving them a 60× burst advantage over the documented contract; `sub_unit_refill_rate_takes_multiple_seconds_per_token` drives a `0.1/s` rate through paused-time advances (5 s → 0.5 tokens, below threshold; 10 s total → 1.0 tokens, above threshold) and asserts the fractional-token accumulator preserves the partial refill between sub-threshold acquire attempts; a regression that coerced `tokens` to `u64` (losing the fractional part on every refill) would make every sub-unit-rate bucket reject forever. Suite grows from 6 to 14 tests; zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only the public `TokenBucket::new` constructor, `try_acquire`, and (for the mutex-poison case) the crate-private `state` field accessible from the inline test module. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-core::sanitize` — C0 control-character sweep across `0x00..=0x1F` + DEL (0x7F), C1 control-character sweep across `0x80..=0x9F` with the 0x9B CSI-introducer carve-out, CSI edge cases (intermediate-only body at EOF, final byte immediately after `[` with no parameters, consecutive ESC introducers producing independent replacements, lone C1 introducer at EOF), Unicode Format-category (Cf) pass-through by design as the documented scope-limit of `char::is_control()`, idempotence under double-application, worst-case log-forging with newline + CSI fully neutralized, and a cross-cutting "no raw control introducer in output" black-box invariant across a mixed corpus** — `crates/rustgate-core/src/sanitize.rs` is the single defensive choke-point between attacker-controlled strings (certificate CN / OU, NATS subject fragments, error-message payloads, any user-supplied field that flows into `tracing::info!(field = %value, …)`) and the structured-log output: it replaces every C0 / C1 control character with `U+FFFD` and fully consumes both the C0 `ESC [ … final` and the C1 `\u{9B} … final` CSI sequences (per ECMA-48 §5.4) so that SGR parameters like `[31m` cannot survive as printable bytes into a log line that looks styled to an operator reading `less -R`. Before this PR the inline suite (11 tests) covered the happy-path newline / control-char / CSI / truncated-CSI / ESC-without-bracket cases plus one log-forging regression test, but it left a material set of defensive invariants unpinned that a silent refactor could break. The one that matters most is **the strict-inequality boundary on the "final byte" of a CSI body combined with the consecutive-introducer independence contract**: the implementation uses `while let Some(&next) = chars.peek() { match next as u32 { 0x20..=0x3F => consume, 0x40..=0x7E => consume + break, _ => break } }`, and both branches of that match matter — a regression that required at least one parameter byte before accepting a final would loop forever on `\x1B[Xhello` (final immediately after `[`), and a regression that made the first ESC enter a "CSI mode" that consumed subsequent ESCs as parameter bytes would silently swallow log content. Ten new inline tests close the gaps. **C0 / C1 control-byte sweep (two)**: `all_c0_control_chars_replaced_including_del` loops `0x00..=0x1F` and asserts every single C0 byte sanitizes to `U+FFFD`, then separately asserts `0x7F` (DEL, Unicode General_Category = Cc even though it sits outside the C0 range) is also replaced — a regression that carved an exception for e.g. `\t` (tab, 0x09, which some log parsers tolerate) would be caught here across the full range in one pass rather than requiring a per-byte feature test; `all_c1_control_chars_except_0x9b_csi_introducer_are_replaced` loops `0x80..=0x9F` with a `continue` on `0x9B` (the C1 CSI introducer, handled by the dedicated introducer arm that replaces + consumes body) and asserts every other byte in the C1 range produces the replacement, which is the only way to discover a regression that narrowed the `is_control()` match to "only below 0x80" and silently let the C1 range through as printable high-bit bytes. **CSI edge cases (four)**: `csi_consumed_when_only_intermediate_bytes_present_and_no_final_at_eof` feeds `\x1B[12;34` (no final byte) and `\u{9B}12;34` (C1 variant) and `\x1B[!"#$` (intermediate-only body with bytes in 0x20-0x2F) — pinning that `consume_csi_body` keeps going across the whole param/intermediate body until EOF even without a final byte, which is the defence against an attacker who deliberately omits the final byte hoping the body will leak; `csi_with_final_byte_immediately_after_bracket_with_no_parameters` feeds `\x1B[Xhello` and asserts the result is `U+FFFD + "hello"` — pinning the zero-parameter-byte branch of the match (`X` is 0x58, directly in the 0x40..=0x7E final-byte range), so a regression that required at least one parameter byte before accepting a final would either loop forever or skip the consumption and leak `X`; `consecutive_esc_introducers_each_produce_independent_replacements` feeds `\x1B\x1B\x1B` and asserts exactly three `U+FFFD` characters, plus a mixed `\x1B\x1B[31mred\x1B` case — pinning that the first ESC does NOT enter a mode that swallows the next ESC as body content, since ESC (0x1B) is OUT of the 0x20..=0x7E body window and `consume_csi_body` correctly breaks on the first non-body byte; `lone_c1_csi_introducer_at_eof_produces_exactly_one_replacement` feeds `\u{9B}` alone and `"prefix\u{9B}"` with nothing after — pinning that `consume_csi_body` on an empty peek queue exits cleanly rather than panicking via an unchecked `chars.next().unwrap()`. **Unicode scope-limit (one)**: `unicode_format_and_bidi_override_characters_pass_through_by_design` feeds `U+202E` RIGHT-TO-LEFT OVERRIDE, `U+FEFF` BYTE ORDER MARK, `U+200E` LEFT-TO-RIGHT MARK, and `U+200F` RIGHT-TO-LEFT MARK and asserts they pass through verbatim — these are Unicode General_Category = Cf (Format) characters which `char::is_control()` explicitly does NOT cover, and the test pins this as the documented scope-limit so a future "stronger sanitizer" refactor that widened to reject Cf would be a conscious deliberate decision rather than silent fallout from a one-line clippy-lint cleanup that shifted to `.is_ascii() && …`. Operationally these characters are legal in UTF-8 identifiers and may legitimately appear in CNs for non-Latin scripts (e.g. Arabic/Hebrew names with bidi controls, Chinese documents with BOM-prefixed encoding headers), so replacing them would break legitimate identity data. **Idempotence (one)**: `sanitize_is_idempotent_on_arbitrary_input` feeds a seven-element corpus (plain, newline, C0 CSI, C1 CSI, mixed control, empty, Unicode) through `sanitize_for_log` twice and asserts `once == twice` — `U+FFFD` itself is a plain Unicode character (not Cc, not a CSI introducer), so re-sanitizing an already-clean string MUST be a no-op. Operational importance: log pipelines that re-sanitize at a downstream stage (e.g. a logging facade that wraps `tracing` with its own belt-and-braces sanitization) must not double-transform the replacement marker. A regression that e.g. replaced `U+FFFD` inputs with two `U+FFFD` outputs would silently bloat every log field on every pipeline hop. **Log-forging deep case (one)**: `log_forging_with_newline_and_csi_is_fully_neutralized` carries the worst-case attacker-controlled CN — a newline-prefixed fake log entry wrapped in green SGR to mimic an "authorized=true" line in a live colourized `less -R` session — and asserts the full neutralized byte-exact expected output plus the defence-in-depth tail assertions that `\n` and `\x1B` do not appear anywhere in the output. A regression that left either the newline or the CSI introducer in place would re-enable the forging channel the module exists to close. **Cross-cutting black-box invariant (one)**: `sanitized_output_contains_no_raw_control_introducers` runs an eight-element diversity corpus (C0 CSI, C1 CSI, `\r\n`, BEL, VT+FF, NUL, TAB, triple-ESC + DEL) and asserts for each output that none of `\x1B`, `\u{9B}`, `\n`, `\r`, `\0`, `\t`, `\x07` remain — this is the stronger corpus-wide contract that a single new bypass path (e.g. a refactor that dropped the `is_control()` catch-all arm in favour of a narrower per-character allowlist that forgot BEL) would fail against multiple corpus entries simultaneously rather than needing a test-per-byte. Suite grows from 11 to 21 tests; zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only the public `sanitize_for_log` through its documented contract. `cargo test -p rustgate-core --lib sanitize::` reports 21/21 passing; full-workspace clippy with the canonical feature-set from `CLAUDE.md` (`nats,enrollment,est,proto,full-detail,metrics,federation,federation-ha,websocket,mqtt,otel,http`) is clean. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-core::masking` — `mask_cert_serial` output-length invariant (exactly 11 chars for every input over 10 bytes, regardless of length), short non-ASCII-input verbatim pass-through without byte-slice panic, `mask_ip` bracketed-IPv6-without-port `after_bracket = ""` branch, non-IP-hostname verbatim return, single-colon-non-IP ambiguous-input defence, `mask_credential` long-input exact-10-char output invariant, short-input exact-8-char-asterisk-only invariant, `hash_cn` lowercase-ASCII-hex output (pins `{b:02x}` format specifier against `{b:02X}` regression), SHA-256 first-6-byte truncation spec conformance cross-checked against independent `sha2::Sha256::digest` computation, case + whitespace variant collision resistance across 7 near-identical CNs, large-input (64-byte, 1 KiB, 64 KiB, 1 MiB) panic-safety, and compile-time owned-`String` return-type pin across all four public functions** — `crates/rustgate-core/src/masking.rs` is the single PII-redaction choke-point between the audit-log layer and the structured-log sinks: every certificate serial, IP address, credential value, and client CN that flows into a `tracing::info!(field = %value, …)` call site goes through one of its four public functions (`mask_cert_serial`, `mask_ip`, `mask_credential`, `hash_cn`) before the bytes ever reach log storage. Before this PR the inline suite (18 tests) covered the happy-path variants and 10-char boundary for each function, but it left a load-bearing set of defensive invariants unpinned. The one that matters most is **the `hash_cn` SHA-256 first-6-byte truncation spec conformance**: the docstring says "SHA-256 truncated to the first 12 hex chars (48 bits)" and the workspace-wide access-log PII guard in `rustgate-transport::router` keys on the resulting correlation token, but the existing suite only pinned that the output is "12 chars of hex" without pinning *which* 12 chars. A regression that swapped to SHA-1 (14 bytes of output → still truncates to 12 hex chars, happy-path tests pass green) or MD5 (16 bytes → truncates the same way) would silently change every correlation token in every deployed operator's dashboard without tripping a single existing test. The new `hash_cn_matches_sha256_first_six_byte_truncation_spec` test recomputes `sha2::Sha256::digest(cn.as_bytes())[..6]` independently, formats the result as `{:02x}` hex for each of six bytes, and asserts byte-for-byte agreement with the module's output across five varied inputs (empty, ASCII, Unicode `\u{4e16}\u{754c}`, long dashed ident). Twelve new inline tests close the gaps in total. **`mask_cert_serial` pins (two)**: `mask_cert_serial_output_is_always_eleven_chars_for_input_longer_than_ten_bytes` sweeps four inputs (11-char, 12-char, 32-char, 1024-char `x`-repeat) and asserts the masked output is ALWAYS exactly 11 characters (`{4 prefix}...{4 suffix}` = 4 + 3 + 4), pinning the output-shape invariant so a regression that widened the prefix/suffix to 6 each (a "better for correlation" refactor that silently discloses more of the serial) trips here; `mask_cert_serial_non_ascii_short_input_returns_verbatim_without_panic` feeds `"café"` (5 bytes), `"日本"` (6 bytes), and `"🔒"` (4 bytes) — all under the 10-byte threshold — to pin that the `<= 10` guard fires BEFORE any byte-slice operation, defending against a regression that re-ordered the guard after byte slicing and panicked on any non-ASCII input at UTF-8 continuation-byte boundaries. **`mask_ip` pins (three)**: `mask_ip_bracketed_ipv6_without_port_preserves_brackets_and_masks_last_group` feeds `"[::1]"` and `"[2001:db8::1]"` (no trailing port), pinning the `after_bracket = ""` branch that the existing `bracketed_ipv6_with_port` test could not reach — a regression that mis-indexed `&ip[bracket_end + 1..]` past len on an empty-suffix input would panic here rather than at a live rejection; `mask_ip_invalid_non_ip_input_returns_verbatim` feeds `"hostname"`, `"localhost"`, `"x"` (no colon, no dot, not bracketed) and asserts verbatim return, pinning the "best-effort helper" contract so a regression that tried to "tolerate" hostnames by silently truncating the last label would trip here; `mask_ip_single_colon_non_ip_returns_verbatim` feeds `"x:y"` and `"no-port:just-text"` (one colon, no dot) and asserts verbatim return — the IPv6 branch requires `matches(':').count() > 1` and the IPv4-with-port branch requires `before.contains('.')`, so neither fires on single-colon-no-dot input; a regression that loosened either guard would mis-classify the input as a mangled IPv4:port and silently emit something like `":***"` into the log. **`mask_credential` pins (two)**: `mask_credential_output_for_long_input_is_exactly_ten_chars` sweeps four inputs (exactly-3-char boundary, medium, 1024-char `x`-repeat, special-char-heavy secret) and asserts the output is ALWAYS exactly 10 characters (`{2 prefix}********` = 2 + 8), pinning the "log column alignment" invariant that downstream grep/awk dashboards assume; a regression that scaled the asterisk count with input length would re-enable length disclosure through the output shape, and this test catches it; `mask_credential_output_for_short_input_is_exactly_eight_chars` pins that empty, 1-char, and 2-char inputs all produce exactly `"********"` (8 characters), preventing a refactor that padded short inputs to 10 chars "for consistency" from disclosing a length signal via whether the output is 8 or 10 chars. **`hash_cn` pins (four)**: `hash_cn_output_is_always_lowercase_ascii_hex` sweeps six varied inputs (ASCII, Unicode `\u{4e16}\u{754c}`, empty, emoji, 256-char `x`-repeat) and asserts every output character is a digit or `a..=f` (explicitly rejecting uppercase `A..=F`) — pins the `{b:02x}` format specifier as the contract so a regression to `{b:02X}` would break downstream case-sensitive log-correlation grep pipelines, a gap the existing `is_ascii_hexdigit`-only check leaves open since that predicate allows BOTH cases; `hash_cn_matches_sha256_first_six_byte_truncation_spec` is the strongest algorithmic test (cross-checks against independent `sha2::Sha256::digest` calls) and is described in the "what matters most" paragraph above; `hash_cn_distinguishes_case_and_whitespace_variants` hashes seven near-identical CNs (`"alice"` / `"Alice"` / `"ALICE"` / `"alice "` / `" alice"` / `"alic"` / `"alicee"`) and collects the 7 outputs into a `HashSet`, asserting the set size equals 7 — pins that the function does NOT normalise (lowercase / trim) the input before hashing, defending against a "helpful" refactor that would silently merge an attacker-controlled CN with trailing whitespace into the legitimate identity's audit trail and let a typoed-CN co-mingle with real activity; `hash_cn_handles_large_inputs_above_sha256_block_boundary_without_panic` sweeps four input sizes (64 bytes = exactly one SHA-256 block, 1 KiB, 64 KiB, 1 MiB) and asserts each produces a valid 12-char hex token without panicking, defending against a regression that tried to stack-allocate a bounded input buffer and overflowed on multi-block inputs. **Compile-time type-surface pin (one)**: `masking_functions_all_return_owned_string_type` uses four `let _: String = …` bindings to pin that every public function returns an owned `String`, not `&str` or `Cow<'_, str>` — a regression to a borrowed return type would tie the output's lifetime to the input string and break every structured-log call site that stores the masked value into a longer-lived `tracing::span` field (the test is a compile-time assertion: if the return type narrowed, the test would fail to compile here rather than at a cascade of failures across transitive call sites). Suite grows from 18 to 30 tests; zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only the four public free functions through their documented contracts. `cargo test -p rustgate-core --lib masking::` reports 30/30 passing; full-workspace clippy with the canonical feature-set from `CLAUDE.md` (`nats,enrollment,est,proto,full-detail,metrics,federation,federation-ha,websocket,mqtt,otel,http`) is clean. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-core::tasks` — `spawn_background` caller-non-blocking invariant under paused-clock (returns in ≤ 100 ms of virtual time even when the spawned future sleeps 60 s), `Send + 'static` future-bound compile-time pin, multi-task independence across 20 concurrent spawns, `()` return-type compile-time pin (NOT `JoinHandle<()>`), `&'static str` name-parameter lifetime compile-time pin, `SubsystemHandle: Send + 'static` bound pin, `SubsystemHandle::new` `Arc::ptr_eq` identity preservation (same Arc the caller passed in, not a re-allocated clone), `pub shutdown` field visibility pin, `stop_subsystem` exact-5-second-timeout virtual-clock bound `[5s, 6s)`, and two-subsystem independence invariant (stopping one does not affect the other)** — `crates/rustgate-core/src/tasks.rs` is the workspace-wide spawn helper every library crate funnels background work through: `spawn_background(name, fut)` wraps `tokio::spawn` with a separately-spawned watcher that logs panics via `tracing::error!(task = name, …)` (closing the "silent panic in dropped JoinHandle" footgun that used to drift across crates), and `SubsystemHandle` + `stop_subsystem` form the graceful-shutdown drain primitive every listener crate (WebSocket, MQTT, plaintext, federation, metrics) uses to exit its accept loop on SIGTERM within a bounded 5-second window. Before this PR the inline suite (8 tests) covered the happy-path spawn/shutdown flows, the panic-log path for both `spawn_background` (panic does not crash runtime, subsequent spawns still work) and `stop_subsystem` (task panics after receiving shutdown → `Ok(Err(_))` arm), the timeout path (stuck task → `Err(_)` arm via paused clock), the already-exited path (sunny-path arm), the `#[derive(Debug)]` pin, and the externally-cloneable `Arc<Notify>` round-trip — but it left the documented API-surface invariants (return type, name lifetime, field visibility, exact timeout value) and the foundational `Send + 'static` + multi-task + multi-subsystem contracts unpinned. Ten new inline tests close those gaps. The one that matters most is **the exact 5-second timeout-value pin plus the `Arc::ptr_eq` identity preservation for `SubsystemHandle::new`**: the module's docstring says "matches the drain deadline used by the binary's graceful-shutdown flow", and the binary's SIGTERM handler has the same 5-second assumption baked in — a regression that shortened `stop_subsystem` to 3 s would silently cut every listener's drain window by 40% (and start producing warnings instead of info logs on legitimate drains), while a regression that widened to 10 s would double the time between SIGTERM and process exit on every deployment. `stop_subsystem_timeout_is_exactly_five_seconds_of_virtual_time` uses `#[tokio::test(start_paused = true)]` + `tokio::time::Instant::now()` + `start.elapsed()` to measure the virtual-time elapsed between `stop_subsystem` entry and exit on a task that calls `std::future::pending().await`, and asserts `elapsed >= 5s && elapsed < 6s` — a tight window that is only satisfied by the literal `Duration::from_secs(5)` inside the `tokio::time::timeout` call. The `Arc::ptr_eq` test (`subsystem_handle_new_stores_same_shutdown_arc_it_was_given`) pins that `SubsystemHandle::new(shutdown, task)` stores the *same* `Arc` the caller passed in, not a re-allocated clone — this is load-bearing because every production listener crate clones the shutdown notifier BEFORE construction (hands the clone into the task body, keeps the original for later external notification); a regression that internally did `Self { shutdown: Arc::new(Notify::new()), task }` (a plausible "defensive copy" refactor) would silently desync the external caller's notify from the task's await and make `stop_subsystem` a no-op on the first drain cycle. The remaining eight tests close the rest of the contract surface. **`spawn_background` pins (five)**: `spawn_background_returns_immediately_without_awaiting_future` uses paused clock + `start.elapsed() <= 100ms` around a spawn whose future sleeps 60 s virtual — a regression that accidentally awaited the inner handle on the hot path (rather than inside the separately-spawned watcher) would block the caller for the full 60 s and trip this test; `spawn_background_accepts_send_static_future_only` uses `fn assert_bounds<F: Future<Output=()> + Send + 'static>(_: F)` to compile-time-pin the future bound, so a narrowing to a named lifetime would fail at the `assert_bounds` call rather than at every far-away spawn site; `spawn_background_runs_many_concurrent_tasks_independently` spawns 20 tasks each incrementing a shared `AtomicUsize` and spins until the counter reaches 20, defending against a regression that funnelled spawns through a shared mutex or accidentally serialised them via a wrapper that awaited the prior handle (the single-task happy-path test would still pass green but this test would either take much longer than the poll window or miss entries); `spawn_background_function_return_type_is_unit` uses `let _: () = spawn_background(…)` to pin that the return type is `()` — a regression to `JoinHandle<()>` would let callers accidentally block on background work (defeating the fire-and-forget contract), and this test trips at compile time on the `let _: ()` binding; `spawn_background_name_parameter_requires_static_str_lifetime` uses a function-pointer coercion (`fn(&'static str, Pin<Box<dyn Future + Send>>) = &spawn_background`-style pin) to pin the name parameter's `&'static str` bound — the name flows into the `tracing::error!` call that fires if the background task panics, which happens at an unknown future time relative to the spawn site, and a narrowing to `&str` would compile at the spawn site but break the tracing macro at the panic-log site (borrowed lifetime does not outlive the spawned watcher). **`SubsystemHandle` + `stop_subsystem` pins (three)**: `subsystem_handle_is_send_and_static` uses `fn assert_bounds<T: Send + 'static>()` parameterised on `SubsystemHandle` — the handle flows across task boundaries (constructed on the main orchestration task, moved into a `stop_subsystem` call site inside a separately-spawned shutdown coordinator), so a refactor that embedded a non-`Send` field or a borrowed reference would break every downstream caller and this test traps it at the bound-helper call; `subsystem_handle_shutdown_field_is_publicly_accessible` uses direct `handle.shutdown` access (not via a getter) plus an `Arc::clone(&handle.shutdown)` to pin the `pub shutdown: Arc<Notify>` field visibility — the server crate's graceful-shutdown flow clones this field to propagate the stop signal into watchdog tasks, and a refactor that narrowed to `pub(crate)` would break server-crate callers with an "invisible-from-rustgate-core" compile error (the test makes that failure mode local to this module); `two_subsystems_stop_independently_without_cross_interference` constructs two handles with distinct shutdown notifiers and distinct task bodies, calls `stop_subsystem` on A first, asserts A has exited AND B has NOT exited (proving no shared internal state), then calls `stop_subsystem` on B and asserts B also exits cleanly — a regression that shared a global `NOTIFY` lock across handles (or re-used a static `Instant` for timeout arithmetic) would cause the second subsystem's await to return prematurely or hang. Suite grows from 8 to 18 tests; zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only the existing public constructors and functions through their documented contracts. `cargo test -p rustgate-core --lib tasks::` reports 18/18 passing; full-workspace clippy with the canonical feature-set from `CLAUDE.md` (`nats,enrollment,est,proto,full-detail,metrics,federation,federation-ha,websocket,mqtt,otel,http`) is clean. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-core::message` — compile-time `Send + Sync + Clone + Debug + 'static` bound pin across `RoutableMessage` / `Metadata` / `Destination` / `MessageType`, exhaustive-match variant-set pins for `MessageType` (seven variants: Position / Chat / Alert / Status / File / Mavlink / Other) and `Destination` (four variants: Group / Direct / Broadcast / Multicast), pairwise-distinct ASCII-lowercase `Destination::kind_str` label invariant, pairwise-distinct `SOURCE_FEDERATION` vs `SOURCE_MAVLINK` constant invariant, `Metadata::is_expired` boundary semantics across `Duration::MAX` TTL (never expires), `Duration::ZERO` TTL (expires immediately after any elapsed time), and within-TTL non-expiry (strict-`>` boundary pin), `Destination::Direct` Display rendering CN not fingerprint (security pin against accidental fingerprint disclosure in audit logs), `MessageType::Display` for `Other` passing special characters through verbatim (no hidden sanitization), and `RoutableMessage::clone` payload `Bytes` reference-count sharing (pointer-identity pin)** — `crates/rustgate-core/src/message.rs` is the workspace-wide envelope every inbound message is normalized into before touching the router: `RoutableMessage { source: Identity, destination: Destination, payload: Bytes, metadata: Metadata }` is constructed by every ingress path (TLS listener, WebSocket, MQTT, plaintext TCP, federation, NATS bridge, MAVLink codec) and consumed by the router's ACL + authz + dedup + position-cache pipeline. `Destination::kind_str` is the low-cardinality Prometheus label for the `rustgate_router_deliver_total{kind="..."}` metric family; `MessageType::Display` and `Destination::Display` feed the `tracing::info!(target: "access", …)` audit log that operators grep for post-incident forensic reconstruction; `Metadata::is_expired` gates the position-cache eviction sweep that runs every 15 s. Before this PR the inline suite (14 tests) covered the basic Display rendering for each variant, the `kind_str` labels, the Debug-format smoke test, and three `is_expired` cases (past TTL, within-TTL, None TTL, future `received_at` treated as fresh) — but it left the compile-time thread-safety + cross-task-movement bounds unpinned, the variant-set closed-enum contracts open to silent addition drift (a new `MessageType::Custom` variant added without a corresponding routing-rule update would pass all existing tests green while breaking downstream consumers at runtime), the boundary cases around `Duration::MAX` / `Duration::ZERO` / strict-`>` unpinned (an inclusive-`>=` refactor would silently expire every freshly-received message at `received_at.elapsed() == ttl`, which on systems with coarse-grained clocks is every message), the `SOURCE_*` constant set open to silent co-mingling (a regression that set `SOURCE_MAVLINK = "federation"` for code-path convenience would silently mix MAVLink telemetry into federation audit streams), the Direct-renders-CN-not-fingerprint security pin open to silent disclosure (a "let's just log the whole Identity" Display refactor would leak cert fingerprints into every access log), the no-hidden-sanitization pin on `MessageType::Other` open (a sanitize-on-Display refactor would break downstream round-trip parsing), and the `Bytes` zero-copy-clone invariant on `RoutableMessage::clone` open (a `Bytes::copy_from_slice` refactor would silently 10-100× the memory cost of multicast dispatch). Eleven new inline tests close those gaps. **The one that matters most is the `Metadata::is_expired` triple-boundary pin** (`Duration::MAX` + `Duration::ZERO` + strict-`>`): the position-cache sweep task calls `is_expired` on every cached entry every 15 s across the full set of active clients (typically 10² to 10⁴ entries), and an inclusive-`>=` regression on the strict-greater boundary would expire every entry exactly when `received_at.elapsed() == ttl`, which at the 1 s TTL used by CoT position updates produces cache-thrashing eviction storms under steady-state load — the three new tests (`metadata_is_expired_with_duration_max_ttl_never_expires`, `metadata_is_expired_with_zero_ttl_after_brief_wait_returns_true`, `metadata_is_expired_boundary_is_strictly_greater`) together pin the full shape of the comparison: `Duration::MAX` is overflow-safe and never returns true; `Duration::ZERO` expires on the first sweep after any elapsed time; and within-TTL non-expiry holds through `received_at.elapsed() < ttl` without flipping to true at equality. The remaining eight tests close the other contract surfaces. **Compile-time bound pins (one test)**: `routable_message_and_subtypes_are_send_sync_clone_debug_static` uses `fn assert_bounds<T: Send + Sync + Clone + std::fmt::Debug + 'static>()` parameterised on all four types (`RoutableMessage`, `Metadata`, `Destination`, `MessageType`) — every routing hop moves these types across task boundaries (from listener task into router task via `mpsc::channel`, from router task into per-session dispatcher via `spawn`, into store-and-forward redb-backed queue serialisation), so a refactor that embedded a non-`Send` field (raw pointer, `Rc`, `RefCell`) or a borrowed reference would break every downstream caller and this test traps it locally at the bound-helper call. **Exhaustive-match variant-set pins (two tests)**: `message_type_exhaustive_match_pins_current_seven_variant_set` and `destination_exhaustive_match_pins_current_four_variant_set` both use no-wildcard `match` statements through every variant — adding a new variant without updating the match forces a compile-time failure at this test site, surfacing the change at a place where the reviewer is primed to audit the corresponding routing-rule / ACL / metric-label / audit-log updates rather than letting it land silently through a `_ => unreachable!()` catch-all buried in the router. **Label + constant distinctness pins (two tests)**: `destination_kind_str_values_are_pairwise_distinct_ascii_lowercase` uses a `HashSet` pairwise-distinct check plus per-label ASCII-lowercase / non-empty / no-whitespace invariants on `group` / `direct` / `broadcast` / `multicast` — a regression that returned the same label for two variants would silently merge Prometheus counters across different routing flows; a regression that introduced uppercase or whitespace would break downstream SIEM-grep queries keyed on the exact label bytes. `source_protocol_constants_are_pairwise_distinct_ascii_lowercase` applies the same invariant to `SOURCE_FEDERATION` and `SOURCE_MAVLINK` — a regression that reused the same string for both would silently co-mingle federation traffic with MAVLink traffic in every audit log, echo-prevention filter, and downstream analytics consumer. **Display-output security + semantic pins (two tests)**: `destination_display_direct_renders_cn_not_fingerprint` constructs a `Destination::Direct(identity)` with `cn = "alice"` and a distinctive `fingerprint = "SECRET_FINGERPRINT"`, then asserts the rendered output equals exactly `"direct:alice"` AND does NOT contain the literal substring `"SECRET_FINGERPRINT"` — a refactor that "helpfully" included the fingerprint for debugging would leak cert fingerprints into every access log at every direct-delivery event and the test traps that before the next log rotation. `message_type_display_other_passes_through_special_chars_verbatim` round-trips `MessageType::Other("a:b")`, `MessageType::Other("with space")`, `MessageType::Other("")`, `MessageType::Other("UPPER")`, `MessageType::Other("digit1")`, `MessageType::Other("x,y,z")` through `to_string()` and asserts the `"other:"` prefix is preserved AND the payload is passed through exactly — a sanitize-on-Display refactor would break every downstream `MessageType::Other` round-trip parser (and every operator's muscle-memory `rg 'other:'` grep). **Zero-copy-clone invariant (one test)**: `routable_message_clone_shares_payload_bytes_ref_count` constructs a `RoutableMessage` with `payload = Bytes::from_static(&[…])`, clones it, and asserts `msg.payload.as_ptr() == cloned.payload.as_ptr()` — `Bytes` is the workspace-wide zero-copy payload type and every multicast dispatch relies on clone being pointer-copy + refcount-bump rather than a deep memcpy; a refactor that introduced a `Bytes::copy_from_slice` along the clone path would silently multiply the memory cost of broadcasting a 10 KiB message to 100 recipients from 10 KiB + 100 refcount-bumps to 1 MiB of heap allocation, and this test traps it at compile-free pointer identity. Suite grows from 14 to 25 tests; zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only the existing public constructors, trait impls, and associated functions through their documented contracts. `cargo test -p rustgate-core --lib message::` reports 25/25 passing; full-workspace clippy with the canonical feature-set from `CLAUDE.md` (`nats,enrollment,est,proto,full-detail,metrics,federation,federation-ha,websocket,mqtt,otel,http`) is clean. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-core::peer_id` — compile-time `Send + Sync + Clone + Debug + 'static` bound pin on both `PeerId` and `ServerName` newtypes, trait-qualified exercise of BOTH `PartialEq<str>` and `PartialEq<&str>` impls that the existing suite resolved away at inference time, accessor-agreement pin (`as_str` / `AsRef::as_ref` / `Borrow::borrow` / `Display`) with pointer-identity on the three `&str` accessors (zero-copy contract), `#[serde(transparent)]` wire-shape pin against silent config-format drift when the newtype appears nested in a struct field, `PeerId` ↔ `ServerName` distinct-HashMap-keyspace invariant (newtype nominal distinctness), empty-string / whitespace-only construction without validation (explicit no-validation contract pin), Unicode / control-char / JSON-escape round-trip verbatim through constructor → Display → serde → constructor, and `Hash`/`Eq` consistency pin via `DefaultHasher`** — `crates/rustgate-core/src/peer_id.rs` is the workspace-wide federation-peer identifier. `PeerId` and `ServerName` are newtypes wrapping `String` with `#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq, Eq, Hash, serde::Serialize, serde::Deserialize)]` + `#[serde(transparent)]` plus hand-rolled `Display`, `From<String>`, `From<&str>`, `AsRef<str>`, `Borrow<str>`, `PartialEq<str>`, `PartialEq<&str>`. They are constructed on the federation config-load path from peer-list TOML / JSON / Consul-KV / etcd-KV entries and flow across every federation task boundary: the main orchestration task holds the peer map, per-peer gRPC connector tasks clone a `PeerId` into their shutdown-watcher, the HA registry task serialises `ServerName` over NATS-KV with a fencing-token, and the heartbeat reconciler task reads both types out of an `ArcSwap<HashMap<PeerId, PeerInfo>>`. The `Borrow<str>` impl is load-bearing on the hot path for `HashMap<PeerId, _>::get("peer-1")` to avoid allocating a temporary `PeerId` per lookup; the `Display` impl is the rendering used in every `tracing::info!(peer_id = %peer, …)` audit-log emission; the `#[serde(transparent)]` attribute is the wire-format contract for every hand-rolled peer config file an operator might maintain (a regression that dropped the attribute would change every config-file JSON shape from `"peer-1"` to `{"0":"peer-1"}` and break startup parsing across every deployment that pins config version). Before this PR the inline suite (18 tests) covered the happy-path Display / as_str / into_inner / `From<String>` / `From<&str>` / Clone / serde round-trip / HashMap lookup via `Borrow<str>` for both types, and a vacuous "newtypes are not interchangeable" compile-time-intent-documenting stub — but it left the foundational `Send + Sync + 'static` bounds unpinned (every federation task-boundary crossing relies on these), left both `PartialEq<str>` impls listed in the source wholly unexercised at runtime (the existing equality tests used `PeerId == PeerId` which hits the derived `PartialEq<PeerId>`, not the two hand-rolled string impls), left the accessor-agreement + zero-copy invariant unpinned (a refactor that had `AsRef::as_ref` return a trimmed / case-normalized slice would silently diverge the ACL-match path from the Display path), left the nested-struct serde-transparent contract unpinned (the existing round-trip asserted JSON == `"\"peer-1\""` for a bare PeerId, which would also pass if `#[serde(transparent)]` were dropped in favour of a hand-rolled impl — but the nested-struct case would fail), left the cross-type HashMap keyspace distinctness unchecked, left the explicit no-validation contract unpinned, and left the Unicode / control-char / JSON-escape round-trip unpinned. Eleven new inline tests close those gaps. **The two that matter most are the `PartialEq<str>` / `PartialEq<&str>` trait-qualified exercise and the `Send + Sync + 'static` compile-time bound pin**: the two string-equality impls are listed as distinct items in the source (`self.0 == other` vs `self.0 == *other`) and neither was exercised at runtime — every prior test used `PeerId == PeerId` which hits the derived `PartialEq<PeerId>`, and `peer == "literal"` in source code resolves to `PartialEq<&str>` at inference time leaving the bare `PartialEq<str>` uncalled. `peer_id_partial_eq_with_str_and_str_ref_impls_both_hold` uses fully-qualified syntax (`<PeerId as PartialEq<str>>::eq(&peer, "peer-1")` and `<PeerId as PartialEq<&str>>::eq(&peer, &"peer-1")`) to unambiguously exercise each impl — a regression that collapsed the two into a single blanket, or that "cleaned up what looks like a duplicate", would silently change the inference behaviour for generic call sites that bound on `PartialEq<str>` without the `&`, and this test traps that locally. `peer_id_and_server_name_are_send_sync_clone_debug_static` uses `fn assert_bounds<T: Send + Sync + Clone + std::fmt::Debug + 'static>()` parameterised on both types — a refactor that embedded a non-`Send` field (raw pointer, `Rc`, `RefCell`) or a borrowed reference would break every federation task-boundary crossing downstream and this test traps it at the bound-helper call site rather than at the first far-away `tokio::spawn`. The remaining nine tests close the rest of the contract surface. **Accessor agreement (two tests)**: `peer_id_accessor_methods_all_agree_on_byte_content` and `server_name_accessor_methods_all_agree_on_byte_content` each construct one value, extract a `&str` via four independent paths (`as_str`, `<_ as AsRef<str>>::as_ref`, `<_ as Borrow<str>>::borrow`, `Display::to_string`), and assert all four yield the same bytes — plus the three `&str` accessors all point into the same underlying `String` via `std::ptr::eq` on their `.as_ptr()` values. A refactor that had `AsRef::as_ref` return a defensively-copied slice would break the zero-copy hot-path contract; a refactor that had `Display` prepend a type name would break the existing `tracing::info!(peer_id = %peer, …)` log-grep invariant. **Serde-transparent nested-struct pin (two tests)**: `peer_id_serde_is_transparent_when_nested_in_a_struct_field` and its ServerName counterpart each wrap the newtype in an enclosing `Outer { peer: PeerId, count: u32 }` struct and assert the serialised JSON is exactly `{"peer":"peer-x","count":7}` (not `{"peer":{"0":"peer-x"},"count":7}`) — the existing bare-round-trip test passes regardless of whether `#[serde(transparent)]` is present, because serde_json happens to produce `"peer-1"` for a tuple-struct-with-one-field even without the attribute in some configurations; the nested-struct case is the discriminating one. **HashMap-keyspace distinctness (one test)**: `peer_id_and_server_name_keep_distinct_hashmap_keyspaces` constructs two `HashMap`s — `HashMap<PeerId, _>` and `HashMap<ServerName, _>` — inserts identical underlying-string values into each, and asserts the lookups return the per-map value (proving the two keyspaces never collide because of the nominal-distinct types). **Empty-string + whitespace pin (one test)**: `peer_id_allows_empty_string_construction_without_validation` pins that the constructor is a type-level tag, not a semantic gate — a well-meaning refactor that added `if id.is_empty() { panic! }` would panic at federation startup on any config with an inadvertent empty peer string, while the existing suite would still pass. **Unicode / special-char round-trip (one test)**: `peer_id_round_trips_unicode_and_special_characters_verbatim` uses a carefully-chosen input combining a non-ASCII lock emoji, Greek letters, CR/LF control characters, and JSON-escape metacharacters (`"`, `'`, `\`) to pin that none of constructor / Display / serde / accessor performs hidden normalization or escaping — a regression that added `.nfc()` on the constructor (well-meaning for ACL-match parity but wrong for an opaque identifier tag) would silently corrupt peer IDs on the wire at every config load. **Hash/Eq consistency (one test)**: `peer_id_hash_is_consistent_with_equality` explicitly hashes two equal PeerIds through `DefaultHasher` and asserts their `finish()` values agree — a regression that hand-rolled `Hash` while keeping the derived `PartialEq` (or vice versa) would silently break `HashMap<PeerId, _>` lookups (keys present but never found again) on every federation connection cycle while leaving every other test green. Suite grows from 18 to 29 tests; zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only the existing public constructors, trait impls, and derived bounds through their documented contracts. `cargo test -p rustgate-core --lib peer_id::` reports 29/29 passing; full-workspace clippy with the canonical feature-set from `CLAUDE.md` (`nats,enrollment,est,proto,full-detail,metrics,federation,federation-ha,websocket,mqtt,otel,http`) is clean. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-core::error` — compile-time `Error + Send + Sync + 'static` bound pin across all three `TransportError` / `ConfigError` / `RustgateError` enums, `&dyn std::error::Error` trait-object coercion pin (the path every `tracing::error!(err = %err, …)` macro expansion and `anyhow::Error::from` call site relies on), `From<io::Error>` routing discriminator (top-level `io::Error` converts to `RustgateError::Io` NOT `TransportError::Bind`, which needs `addr` context), error-chain termination depth pin (Transport(Bind { source: io }) walks to exactly 2 layers — transparent → io::Error → None — no silent box-wrap drift), `io::ErrorKind` round-trip preservation via downcast through the `Io` variant (`WouldBlock` / `ConnectionReset` / `NotFound` / … kinds survive), exhaustive-match variant-set pins for all three enums (TransportError seven variants, ConfigError four, RustgateError three), `From<TransportError>` addr-context preservation through the transparent wrap, and `Result<T>` type-alias identity pin (bidirectional bare assignment between `Result<u32>` and `std::result::Result<u32, RustgateError>` with no conversion)** — `crates/rustgate-core/src/error.rs` is the workspace-wide error hierarchy every crate funnels transport and configuration failures through: `TransportError` (seven variants: Bind / TlsHandshake / NoCertificate / EmptyCertChain / MissingCommonName / MissingKeyUsage / Other) is the taxonomy every TLS listener / federation connector / enrollment server returns on socket bind, handshake failure, or X.509 identity-extraction error; `ConfigError` (four variants: PemParse / EmptyPem / TlsVersion / Other) is the taxonomy every PEM-loading / TLS-config-building call site returns on malformed input; `RustgateError` (three variants: Transport / Config / Io) is the top-level `?`-target every `pub fn` in the core crate is declared as `Result<T>` = `Result<T, RustgateError>`. `#[error(transparent)]` on Transport and Config means their Display and `source()` forward to the inner variant; `#[error("io: {0}")]` on Io prefixes; `#[from]` attributes wire the three auto-conversions from the inner types. Before this PR the inline suite (27 tests) covered the Display + `source()` surface for every variant (incl. static-text pins, prefix pins, transparent-Display pins, no-source pins), the three `?`-propagation paths, the Debug variant-name pin, and the Result-alias functional round-trip — but it left the foundational `Send + Sync + 'static` bounds unpinned across all three enums (every spawned-task `Result` return and every `Box<dyn Error + Send + Sync + 'static>` coercion at a `tracing::error!` call site relies on these), left the `&dyn Error` trait-object coercion path unpinned (the only way a `tracing::error!(err = %err)` macro can render a `&dyn Error` is through this coercion, and a regression that accidentally dropped the `thiserror::Error` derive would only surface at every logging call site in the binary rather than locally), left the critical `From<io::Error>` discriminator unpinned (a "helpful" refactor that added `#[from] io::Error` to `TransportError::Bind` would silently route top-level I/O errors into `Bind` with an empty `addr` string — the audit log would then claim a bind failure at address `""` at every config-read-failure path), left the error-chain termination depth unpinned (a refactor that box-wrapped the `io::Error` inside an intermediate layer would extend the chain from 2 to 3 and break `anyhow::Error`'s default source-walk rendering plus every matcher that counts), left the `io::ErrorKind` downcast round-trip unpinned (the store-forward retry loop and CRL reloader both match on the kind via `source().downcast_ref::<io::Error>().kind()` to decide retry policy — a regression that wrapped the `io::Error` in a new error type with preserved Display would silently degrade every kind-specific retry to the generic fallback), left the exhaustive-match closed-enum contracts open to silent additions (a new `TransportError::CertRevoked` variant added without updating downstream handlers would slip through every existing `match t` via a buried `_ =>` arm), left the addr-context preservation through the transparent wrap unverified (only Display equality was pinned, not structural preservation of the `addr` field), and left the `Result<T>` type-alias identity contract implicit (the alias must be type-equal to `std::result::Result<T, RustgateError>` so a refactor that changed it to a newtype wrapper would break every downstream `?`-propagation site). Ten new inline tests close those gaps. **The three that matter most are the `Send + Sync + 'static` bound pin, the `From<io::Error>` discriminator, and the `io::ErrorKind` downcast round-trip**: `all_error_enums_satisfy_error_send_sync_static_bound` uses `fn assert_bounds<T: std::error::Error + Send + Sync + 'static>()` parameterised on each of `TransportError`, `ConfigError`, `RustgateError` — every crate in the workspace has `pub fn foo() -> anyhow::Result<T>` call sites that `?`-propagate from these enums via `anyhow::Error::from(err)` which internally requires the `Error + Send + Sync + 'static` bound; a refactor that embedded a non-`Send` field (raw `Rc`, `RefCell`, or a borrowed `&'a str` in a new variant) or a `'static`-narrowing borrow would break every downstream anyhow-wrap at a far-away call site, and this test traps it locally at the bound-helper call. `io_error_converts_to_rustgate_io_variant_not_transport_bind` constructs an `io::Error`, runs it through the `From<io::Error>` path, and matches on `RustgateError::Io` — pinning that a top-level `?` on an io::Error gives you `RustgateError::Io(err)` with the `"io: "` prefix, NOT `TransportError::Bind` (which requires the `addr` field that a bare io::Error cannot supply); this discrimination is the entire point of having both a `RustgateError::Io` variant and a `TransportError::Bind { addr, source }` variant, and a regression that added `#[from] io::Error` to `Bind` would silently emit an empty-addr audit event on every config-read-failure path. `rustgate_io_preserves_io_error_kind_through_downcast` wraps an `io::Error::new(WouldBlock, …)` in `RustgateError::Io`, walks `source()` to retrieve the inner, downcasts to `&io::Error`, and asserts `.kind() == WouldBlock` — a regression that box-wrapped the inner would still preserve Display but lose the downcast, and every kind-sensitive retry classifier in the workspace (store-forward drain, CRL reloader backoff, federation connector) would degrade to its generic fallback. The remaining seven tests close the rest of the contract surface. **Trait-object coercion (one test)**: `errors_coerce_to_dyn_error_trait_object` creates an `&dyn std::error::Error` binding for each of the three enums; a regression that dropped the `thiserror::Error` derive on any one enum would fail this coercion at compile time rather than at every `tracing::error!(err = %err)` call site in the binary. **Error-chain depth (one test)**: `rustgate_error_source_chain_terminates_at_io_error_depth_two` walks `top.source().and_then(|e| e.source())` on a `Transport(Bind { source: io })` value and asserts the second hop is `None` — pinning that the transparent wrap does not accidentally introduce an intermediate layer. **Exhaustive-match variant-set pins (three tests)**: `transport_error_exhaustive_variant_match_pins_current_seven_variant_set` + `config_error_exhaustive_variant_match_pins_current_four_variant_set` + `rustgate_error_exhaustive_variant_match_pins_current_three_variant_set` each loop over a hand-populated array containing one of every current variant and dispatch through a no-wildcard `match` — adding a new variant without updating the match forces a compile-time failure at this site, surfacing the change where the reviewer is primed to audit the corresponding audit-log / retry-classifier / operator-facing-message-table updates. **Addr-context preservation (one test)**: `rustgate_from_transport_bind_preserves_addr_context` asserts that `RustgateError::from(TransportError::Bind { addr: "192.0.2.1:4443", … })` preserves `addr` bit-for-bit through the transparent wrap — a refactor that normalized the addr (stripped a port, replaced private IPs with `"<redacted>"`) on the way into the top-level error would break the audit-log invariant that bind-failure events carry the exact addr the operator configured. **Result-alias identity (one test)**: `result_alias_is_identical_to_std_result_with_rustgate_error` assigns a `Result<u32>` binding to a `std::result::Result<u32, RustgateError>` binding and vice versa without any conversion — pinning that the alias is identity not a newtype; a regression that changed the alias to a wrapper struct would break this bare assignment and every downstream `?` propagation that crosses an alias boundary. Suite grows from 27 to 37 tests; zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only the existing public error enums, their derived / hand-rolled trait impls, and the `#[from]` / `#[error(…)]` attribute machinery through their documented contracts. `cargo test -p rustgate-core --lib error::` reports 37/37 passing; full-workspace clippy with the canonical feature-set from `CLAUDE.md` (`nats,enrollment,est,proto,full-detail,metrics,federation,federation-ha,websocket,mqtt,otel,http`) is clean. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-core::identity` — compile-time `Send + Sync + 'static` bound pin on `Identity` / `AttrValue` / `IdentityError`, exhaustive-match variant-set pins for both `AttrValue` (four variants: Str / Int / Bool / Set) and `IdentityError` (four variants: EmptyCn / CnTooLong / CnContainsControlChars / EmptyFingerprint), `AttrValue` serde wire-format pin (`{"type":"…","value":…}` tag/content discriminator + snake_case renaming, byte-for-byte JSON for each variant through round-trip), `AttrValue::Set` caller-supplied-order + duplicate preservation (non-normalising constructor contract), `IdentityError::CnTooLong` Display carries both actual length AND the `MAX_CN_LENGTH` constant (operator-facing diagnostic invariant), `MAX_CN_LENGTH = 256` literal-value pin with docstring-rationale (above RFC 5280's 64 to accommodate compound plaintext CNs), `Identity::plaintext` also enforces the length cap (shared-validation invariant against a split-`validate_cn` refactor), `Identity` builder-chain field preservation across `with_tenant` / `with_spiffe_id` / `with_attributes`, `Identity::clone` shares the `attributes: Arc<BTreeMap<…>>` via `Arc::ptr_eq` + `Arc::strong_count` (zero-copy contract against a bare-map refactor), `Identity` cert-rotation distinctness (two identities sharing a CN but differing by fingerprint are NOT equal, load-bearing for the authz-cache HashMap discriminator), and `Hash`/`Eq` consistency via `DefaultHasher`** — `crates/rustgate-core/src/identity.rs` is the workspace-wide identity type that every authenticated connection carries through the router / authz / ACL / audit / quota / dedup pipeline: `Identity { cn, fingerprint, groups, tenant, spiffe_id, attributes: Arc<BTreeMap<String, AttrValue>> }` is constructed on the TLS-handshake path from the presented client certificate (CN from subject, fingerprint via SHA-256, groups from OU fields, tenant from O field, spiffe_id from URI SAN, attributes from the full SAN bag) or on the plaintext-session path with a synthetic CN like `udp-anonymous-127.0.0.1:5600`. `AttrValue` is the unit of authorization context the RBAC/ABAC engine consumes — stored in the identity's attribute bag, flowed into every Cedar policy evaluation, serialised into the authz-decision-cache key, and persisted across process restarts in the authz-reloader's hash-chained policy file. `IdentityError` is the construction-time validation taxonomy surfaced to operators in TLS-handshake audit logs when a client presents a malformed CN. Before this PR the inline suite (16 tests) covered the happy-path `Identity::new` / `Identity::plaintext` construction, `is_member_of` group membership, the four CN-rejection paths (empty / too-long / control-chars across null/newline/tab/escape), the accept-Unicode / accept-special-chars paths, the length-boundary happy-case at exactly MAX_CN_LENGTH, plaintext rejection of empty CN and control chars, and the Display-shows-cn invariant — but it left twelve foundational contracts unpinned that a refactor could silently break. Twelve new inline tests close those gaps. **The three that matter most are the `AttrValue` serde wire-format pin, the `MAX_CN_LENGTH = 256` literal-value pin, and the `Identity::clone` Arc-sharing pin**: `attrvalue_serde_wire_format_is_tagged_snake_case` pins the exact JSON shape (`{"type":"str","value":"hi"}`, `{"type":"int","value":-7}`, `{"type":"bool","value":true}`, `{"type":"set","value":["a","b"]}`) for every variant, with round-trip back to the parsed enum — `#[serde(tag = "type", content = "value", rename_all = "snake_case")]` is the wire contract every upstream attribute provider, every Cedar policy's attribute lookup, every authz-cache serialised-decision key, and every policy-file hash-chained persistence format depends on, and a regression that changed to default adjacent-tagged or untagged serde layout (or to `rename_all = "camelCase"` for consistency with some other config file) would silently break every consumer at the first external-boundary crossing. `max_cn_length_constant_is_256_as_documented` pins the literal value `256` — the module comment explicitly documents that 256 is chosen above RFC 5280's 64-char cap to accommodate compound plaintext CNs like `udp-anonymous-127.0.0.1:5600`; a regression that "tightened" the cap to RFC parity would reject every plaintext CN the transport layer synthesises for UDP / plaintext-TCP sessions and produce silent connection-rejection storms on production deployments. `identity_attributes_arc_is_shared_not_copied_on_clone` uses `Arc::ptr_eq(&id.attributes, &cloned.attributes)` plus `Arc::strong_count(&id.attributes) == 2` to pin the zero-copy clone contract — the authz cache stores millions of per-subject decisions keyed by identity clone (each session's Identity is cloned into the cache as part of the decision key), and a refactor that changed the field to a bare `BTreeMap<String, AttrValue>` would silently deep-copy the attribute map on every clone and 10-100× the memory cost of the cache hot path without changing any observable behaviour outside memory pressure. The remaining nine tests close the rest of the contract surface. **Compile-time bound pin (one test)**: `identity_attrvalue_and_identity_error_are_send_sync_static` uses `fn assert_bounds<T: Send + Sync + 'static>()` parameterised on all three types — every TLS-handshake spawn site returns `Result<Identity, IdentityError>` from a spawned task and the authz cache shards embed AttrValue across shard boundaries; a refactor that embedded a non-`Send` field would only surface at those spawn sites without this local pin. **Exhaustive-match variant-set pins (two tests)**: `attrvalue_exhaustive_match_pins_current_four_variant_set` and `identity_error_exhaustive_match_pins_current_four_variant_set` each loop over a hand-populated array containing one of every current variant and dispatch through a no-wildcard `match` — adding a new variant without updating the authz policy-evaluator (AttrValue) or the operator-facing error-message table (IdentityError) would slip through a buried `_ =>` arm, and these tests surface the change where the reviewer is primed to audit the downstream consumer updates. **`AttrValue::Set` non-normalisation (one test)**: `attrvalue_set_preserves_caller_supplied_order_and_duplicates` constructs `Set(vec!["bravo", "alpha", "bravo", "charlie"])` and asserts the items round-trip verbatim through the pattern-match — the module doc explicitly documents that `Set` is a `Vec<String>` (not `BTreeSet`) so callers get deterministic serialization order AND are responsible for sort+dedup themselves; a well-meaning refactor that added `.sort() + .dedup()` inside the variant constructor would silently break every caller using Set for ordered-list semantics (e.g. SAN DNS entries where the first entry carries SNI-preference signal). **CnTooLong Display pin (one test)**: `identity_error_cn_too_long_display_includes_actual_len_and_max` asserts the Display output contains both the actual length AND the `MAX_CN_LENGTH` constant — a refactor that omitted the constant for "cleaner" log output would force operators to dig into the source code to find the cap, breaking the self-diagnostic audit-log invariant. **Plaintext validation parity (one test)**: `plaintext_rejects_cn_too_long` pins that the shared `validate_cn` helper's length cap applies to plaintext constructors too — a refactor that split validation (applying the length cap only in `new` on the premise that "plaintext sessions don't go through TLS so the 64-char X.509 limit doesn't apply") would let plaintext CNs grow unbounded and breach every log-size assumption downstream. **Builder-chain preservation (one test)**: `identity_builder_chain_preserves_prior_fields` constructs an Identity, chains `with_tenant("acme-corp")`, `with_spiffe_id("spiffe://example/alice")`, `with_attributes(map)`, and asserts every field (cn + fingerprint + groups from `new`, plus the three builder overrides) is present in the final value — a refactor that "rebuilt" Self inside a builder (e.g. to gain a `Default` impl for a new field) would silently reset prior builder outputs. **Cert-rotation distinctness (one test)**: `identity_with_different_fingerprint_is_not_equal` constructs two identities sharing a CN but differing by fingerprint and asserts they are NOT equal — load-bearing for the `HashMap<Identity, _>` path used by the dedup/authz cache where fingerprint is the discriminator separating two cert-rotation-separated sessions; a "simplification" refactor that made Identity equality CN-only would silently merge the two sessions' cache entries and leak authorization decisions across cert rotations. **Hash/Eq consistency (one test)**: `identity_equal_instances_produce_equal_hash` explicitly hashes two structurally-equal Identity values through `DefaultHasher` and asserts agreement — a regression that hand-rolled `Hash` while keeping the derived `PartialEq` (or vice versa) would silently break every `HashMap<Identity, _>` lookup on every authz-cache hit while leaving every other test green. Suite grows from 16 to 28 tests; zero changes to any production API — every new test drives only the existing public constructors, builder methods, trait impls, and serde attributes through their documented contracts. `cargo test -p rustgate-core --lib identity::` reports 28/28 passing; full-workspace clippy with the canonical feature-set from `CLAUDE.md` (`nats,enrollment,est,proto,full-detail,metrics,federation,federation-ha,websocket,mqtt,otel,http`) is clean. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-transport::config` — `TlsMinVersion::protocol_versions()` static-slice shape pin (Tls12 advertises both TLS 1.2 and 1.3, Tls13 advertises exactly TLS 1.3 only), `TlsMinVersion` and `CrlFileSummary` derive bounds (Copy/Clone/Debug/PartialEq/Eq), `TlsConfig::from_pem` post-construction defaults (TLS 1.2, empty CRLs, stale-warning enabled) and malformed-server-cert rejection, builder chain (`with_crl_warn_stale`, `with_tls_min_version`, `with_crls` empty/unparseable/real-CRL arms), accessor contract (`client_ca_store`, `crls`, `crl_max_age_seconds`), `validate_crl_file` missing-path/empty-file/valid-summary tri-branch, `check_crl_freshness` fresh/expired/garbage/mixed arms, `build_server_config` and `build_reloadable_server_config` both disable TLS session resumption (CVE-2025-23419-class pin on `NoServerSessionStorage`), `build_default_verifier` with-and-without-CRLs, TLS-1.3-only build path, and `is_fips_provider_active` / `is_pq_provider_active` false-under-`ring` accessor pins** — `crates/rustgate-transport/src/config.rs` is the load-bearing mTLS server-config factory (701 LOC) that every listener bootstrap, every SIGHUP reload, and every CRL-age Prometheus gauge flows through. Before this PR the inline suite only had four `TlsMinVersion::from_str_version` parser shapes and three `crl_max_age_seconds` helpers — the builder surface, the session-storage hardening that closes the CVE-2025-23419 class of cached-ticket auth bypasses, the disk-read `validate_crl_file` reload path, and the FIPS/PQ provider accessors were all unpinned. Twenty-six new inline tests close those gaps. Suite grows from nine to thirty-five tests. `cargo test -p rustgate-transport --lib config::tests` reports 35/35 passing; `cargo +nightly fmt --all` clean; full-workspace clippy with the canonical feature-set from CLAUDE.md is clean. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-federation::ha::claims` `OnPeerAvailable` contract, callback Send+Sync+'static bounds, Arc-clone semantics, adoption-jitter distribution, claim-TTL refresh-window invariant, and zero-sentinel guards on every duration constant** — `crates/rustgate-federation/src/ha/claims.rs` is the distributed-ownership glue that lets a multi-pod HA deployment converge on exactly one owner per outbound federation peer: atomic `kv.create(peer_id, pod_id)` wins the claim, a refresh loop re-puts every `REFRESH_INTERVAL_SECS` (10 s) to keep the `CLAIM_TTL_SECS` (30 s) TTL alive, a watch loop detects KV `Delete`/`Purge` operations (pod death → claim expiry), and a jittered `adoption_jitter()` sleep spread across surviving pods prevents a thundering-herd reclaim. Before this PR the inline suite (5 tests: `adopt_delay_is_30s`, `adoption_jitter_is_bounded`, `claim_bucket_name`, `constants_are_reasonable`, `on_claim_lost_type_includes_fencing_token`) covered literal-constant pins and a single-sample jitter bound but left eight defensive invariants unpinned that a refactor could silently break. Eight new inline tests close the gap: `on_peer_available_callback_fires_with_owned_peer_id_string` mirrors the existing `OnClaimLost` test but for the sibling `OnPeerAvailable = Arc<dyn Fn(String) + Send + Sync>` alias — a regression that narrowed the signature to `Fn(&str)` (cheap to type, catastrophic at the `tokio::spawn` call site inside `watch_expired_claims`) would compile through every existing test in the module but trip here via the owned-`String` capture; `on_peer_available_type_satisfies_send_sync_static_bound` and `on_claim_lost_type_satisfies_send_sync_static_bound` use a local `fn assert_bounds<T: Send + Sync + 'static>()` compile-time pin on each alias — both callbacks flow across task boundaries (`OnPeerAvailable` is moved into a `tokio::spawn` block after a jittered sleep, `OnClaimLost` is called from inside the refresh loop's `tokio::select!`), and a regression that dropped `Send`, `Sync`, or narrowed the lifetime would only surface at those spawn sites, not in this module's own harness; `callback_arcs_can_be_cloned_independently_and_fire_shared_closure` pins the `Arc<dyn Fn …>` identity contract — the test captures a shared `Arc<Mutex<u32>>` counter, clones the outer `OnPeerAvailable` handle, asserts `Arc::strong_count == 2` (proving the clone bumps the strong count rather than wrapping in an outer `Arc`), fires both handles with different peer ids, and asserts the counter reaches exactly 2, so a future refactor that changed the alias to a bare `Box<dyn Fn …>` would still satisfy the single-handle functional signature tests but fail at the strong-count/clone assertion; `adoption_jitter_stays_strictly_below_max_across_many_samples` is the 2048-sample bound check — where the existing `adoption_jitter_is_bounded` calls the helper exactly once and asserts below `MAX_ADOPT_JITTER_MS`, this test stresses the modulo arithmetic across a ~2 ms span of `SystemTime::now().subsec_nanos()` values (microsecond clock resolution × 2048 iterations), catching a regression where a refactor that changed the modulus operand order or narrowed to `u32` could produce a single out-of-bounds sample on a subsec_nanos value close to `u32::MAX`; `adoption_jitter_samples_span_more_than_one_distinct_value` is the degeneracy guard — if the helper ever regressed to a constant (deleted modulo, hard-coded zero, or the `subsec_nanos` source accidentally replaced with a field that returns the same value every call) the distinct-value count across 512 sequential samples would collapse to one and fail here, and the assertion is probabilistic in the formal sense but operationally robust because the ~512 µs span between first and last sample at microsecond-or-better clock resolution makes a degenerate collapse astronomically unlikely; `claim_ttl_permits_at_least_two_full_refresh_windows_before_expiry` is the stronger sibling of the existing `TTL > refresh` invariant — the module's lifecycle doc advertises "re-puts every 10 s to keep the 30 s TTL alive", which operationally requires the TTL to survive at least *one* missed refresh (a brief NATS hiccup must not lose the claim), so `CLAIM_TTL_SECS >= 2 * REFRESH_INTERVAL_SECS` is the design-motivated invariant that a future "shorten TTL to 15 s for faster failover" edit would trip here instead of at a live incident; `ha_duration_constants_are_all_strictly_positive` closes the zero-sentinel class — a zero refresh would busy-loop the task, zero TTL expires instantly, zero jitter defeats the thundering-herd mitigation (every pod races at the same instant), and zero adopt delay skips the documented remote-cleanup window, so the test asserts all four `ha/mod.rs` constants (`CLAIM_TTL_SECS`, `REFRESH_INTERVAL_SECS`, `MAX_ADOPT_JITTER_MS`, `ADOPT_DELAY_SECS`) are strictly `> 0` with failure messages that name the operational consequence per constant. Suite grows from 5 to 13 tests; zero changes to production code — every test drives the module's existing crate-private surface through the static helpers and public type aliases. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-nats::serde_compat` — `UNKNOWN_POSITION_ERROR` 1,000,000-metre threshold pin, `is_position_unknown` strictly-greater-or-equal boundary contract (inclusive at exactly 1 000 000 m, exclusive one ULP below), `is_zero_f64` IEEE-754 semantics (treats `-0.0` as zero, rejects NaN and ±∞ and subnormal/tiny-non-zero), `is_zero_i32` boundary-value handling, `map_event_type` four-branch dispatcher exhaustiveness (`a-*` → Position, `b-t-f*` → Chat, `b-a*` → Alert, other `b-*` / non-`a`/`b` → `MessageType::Other(prefix)`), `format_rfc3339` Unix-epoch fidelity + sub-second truncation via `replace_nanosecond(0)`, `from_cot` default-field pinning (`how = "m-g"`, `ce = 50.0`, `le = 30.0`, `version = "2.0"`), zero-position-on-missing-point, 300-second stale fallback when `stale` is absent, TTL-is-honoured round-trip, `time == start` identity preservation, `into_routable` contract surface (`Destination::Broadcast`, `ttl: None` when missing, `source_protocol == "nats"`, empty-group skip, CN derived from UID, classifier agrees with `map_event_type` dispatch, payload bytes pass through byte-for-byte zero-copy), serde rename-and-skip contract (`type` for `event_type`, `trackValid` / `_flow-tags_` / `precisionlocation` / `xmlDetail` renames, `skip_serializing_if` on optional strings + absent detail section + zero `course`/`speed`/false `track_valid`), deserialisation tolerance (minimal JSON with every optional missing + empty-object detail as all-None), and `Send + Sync + Clone + Debug` compile-time bounds on `WireEvent`/`WirePoint`/`WireDetail`/`WireTrack`/`WireRemark`/`WireContact`** — `crates/rustgate-nats/src/serde_compat.rs` is the load-bearing JSON wire-format module every `rustgate` ↔ NATS federation bridge flows through: it defines the `Wire*` structs that round-trip between `RoutableMessage` and the JSON payload published on the JetStream subject, maps CoT-style event-type prefixes into `MessageType` routing classifications, and carries the rename attributes that pin the on-the-wire key names every external consumer (Grafana, downstream rustgate peers, third-party ingest pipelines) depends on to decode. Before this PR the inline suite (five tests) covered a handful of happy-path round-trip cases but left a material set of defensive invariants unpinned — the sort a silent refactor could break without any integration test noticing until a deployed consumer started rejecting payloads. Thirty-seven new inline tests close those gaps. The one that matters most is the `UNKNOWN_POSITION_ERROR` threshold plus the `is_position_unknown` inclusive-at-threshold contract: the production semantics are "if circular-error exceeds one thousand kilometres, treat the position as unknown and skip the NATS publish"; a regression that dropped the threshold to 100 km would silently start filtering valid positions, a regression that flipped the comparison to exclusive-at-threshold would let exactly-1 000 000-m sentinel values through, and a regression that renamed the constant would decouple every downstream consumer's filtering from rustgate's. The new suite pins the constant to its literal value (`1_000_000.0_f64`), the comparison direction (`>=`), and the exact-boundary behaviour on both sides of one ULP. The `is_zero_f64` tests pin the IEEE-754 `-0.0 == 0.0` semantics (which matters because serde's `skip_serializing_if` will omit the field only if the predicate returns true, and a manually-authored `== 0.0` helper that excluded `-0.0` would silently emit `"course": -0.0` into the JSON output when a buggy upstream CoT encoder produced a signed zero) plus the NaN / ±∞ / subnormal rejection paths so a naive `.abs() < EPSILON` refactor would fail here rather than silently flip the serialised output. The `map_event_type` five tests walk all four dispatcher arms against their prefix-boundary cases (including `b-i-*` which falls through to `Other` because only `b-t-f` and `b-a` hit specialised arms, and a regression that added a fifth arm without updating the classifier contract would be caught at the `Other` variant's payload assertion). The `from_cot` five tests pin the default-field contract every downstream consumer reads back — a regression that flipped `how` from `"m-g"` (GPS-derived) to `"h-e"` (human-entered) would silently change every position's provenance metadata, and the 300-second stale fallback is the canonical "how long before we treat this track as aged-out" knob every dashboard keys on. The `into_routable` seven tests pin the cross-subsystem contract surface — `Destination::Broadcast` is what the router's ACL path assumes (a regression to `Destination::Group` would silently flip the authorisation model from broadcast-allowlist to group-based), `source_protocol == "nats"` is the literal string the echo-prevention path grep-compares against (a silent typo to `"NATS"` or `"jetstream"` would bypass echo prevention and create a routing loop), empty-group skip is the "no group, no ACL key" contract the router's group-lookup map relies on, and the payload-byte-for-byte pass-through is what makes the federation-to-NATS path zero-copy via `Bytes`. The serde rename tests (`trackValid`, `_flow-tags_`, `precisionlocation`, `xmlDetail`) pin the load-bearing key-name contracts external CoT-over-NATS consumers decode against — every one of those renames is idiosyncratic (mixed camelCase, underscored-hyphenated, and legacy-lowercase forms chosen to stay compatible with the upstream TAK Protocol wire format) and a refactor that "cleaned them up" to consistent snake_case would silently break every deployed consumer without a single in-process test failure.
