All eight tests pass under `cargo nextest run -p rustgate-core tasks::` and `cargo clippy -p rustgate-core --all-targets --locked -- -D warnings` is clean. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-federation::v2::convert` `ConvertError` surface and XML helper contract** — `crates/rustgate-federation/src/v2/convert/mod.rs` previously had three XML-escape tests but zero coverage for the `ConvertError` error-type hierarchy (`Xml`, `MissingField`, `InvalidField`, `InvalidTimestamp` variants), zero coverage for the `std::error::Error` trait impl, zero coverage for the `From<rustgate_proto::ProtoError>` conversion (which maps `MissingField` 1:1, `InvalidTimestamp` 1:1, and routes every other variant through `Xml(other.to_string())`), and zero coverage for the `write_attr` XML-attribute helper. Eleven new inline tests close that gap: `xml_escape_into_empty_is_no_op` and `xml_escape_into_preserves_non_ascii_bytes_unchanged` pin the low-level byte writer's boundary behaviour and the "only escape the five entities, never lossily transform UTF-8 continuation bytes" contract (a regression here would produce subtly-malformed XML that downstream parsers accept but misinterpret); `xml_escape_string_helper_escapes_all_five_entities` mirrors the existing `xml_escape_special_characters` test through the `String`-based helper so a future refactor that accidentally dropped `&apos;` / `&quot;` from only one code path fails on the first build; `write_attr_emits_name_equals_quoted_value_with_leading_space` pins the exact output format (` name="value"` with a leading space so `build_tag()` can chain attributes without explicit separators); `write_attr_escapes_value_but_not_name` pins that the name is written raw (it is always an internal static) while the value is escaped — a regression that started escaping the name would produce malformed XML that quick-xml's strict validator would reject at the peer; four `convert_error_*_display_renders_*` tests pin the `Display` output for every `ConvertError` variant so log-grep and SIEM ingest rules that key off the prefix tokens ("xml parse error:", "missing required field:", "invalid field:", "invalid timestamp:") stay stable; `convert_error_implements_std_error_trait` compile-time-asserts the `std::error::Error` bound via `fn assert_error<T: Error>(_: &T) {}` so a caller wrapping a `ConvertError` in `anyhow::Error` or `Box<dyn Error>` stays compilable, and pins that `source()` returns `None` on leaf errors (the impl is default-derived); three `proto_error_*_converts_to_*` tests pin the `From<ProtoError>` shape end-to-end — `MissingField("event")` preserves the `&'static str` through the conversion, `InvalidTimestamp("missing T separator")` preserves the owned `String`, and `FrameTooLarge { size: 1 << 24, max: 1 << 20 }` (a representative non-MissingField, non-InvalidTimestamp variant) falls through to `ConvertError::Xml(other.to_string())` with the `Display` rendering preserved. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-authz::cache` — `DecisionCache` sizing, counters, Debug, Clone semantics, dedup invariants, shard formula, and cross-key hash disambiguation** — `crates/rustgate-authz/src/cache.rs` previously had three inline tests (`hit_miss_basic`, `epoch_change_invalidates`, `fifo_evicts_oldest`) covering the sunny-path contracts of the sharded authz decision cache but leaving a significant amount of load-bearing behaviour unpinned: the power-of-two shard rounding in `DecisionCache::new` (which `shard_mask = shards - 1` silently relies on — break the rounding and `shard_idx` stops covering the full shard array), the `Default` sizing constants (`16 × 4096`) that every engine without an explicit cache-size configuration inherits, the custom `Debug` impl that operators rely on for metrics dumps and triage, the `Clone` impl's documented "fresh empty cache with same sizing, counters reset" contract (which exists specifically so cloning an authz engine doesn't smuggle the original's steady-state decisions into the clone), the `insert` early-return on duplicate keys (which must neither overwrite the existing outcome *nor* bump the FIFO position — a silent overwrite would let a later caller flip a cached decision, and a silent FIFO touch would degrade the LRU-ish approximation into something stranger), the `CachedOutcome` round-trip of `rule_id` and `obligations` through the private wire type between `insert` and `get`, the `DecisionKey` Eq/Hash derive's inclusion of the full 128-bit `SubjectHash` (drop `secondary` from the derive and the adversarial-resistance claim in the module docs evaporates silently), the per-key independence of `action` and `resource_hash` in the key identity (fold either out and a Send decision silently satisfies a Receive check — authz escape hatch), the independence of the `hits`/`misses` atomic counters (a regression that bumped the wrong one would only show up as nonsense ratios in prod Grafana), and the `shard_idx` formula's consumption of `secondary` (if the mix dropped `secondary`, an adversary who pins `primary = 0` and `resource_hash = 0` funnels every cache entry into shard 0). Ten new inline tests close those gaps: `new_rounds_shard_count_up_to_next_power_of_two` drives a six-case table (3→4, 5→8, 7→8, 9→16, 16→16, 1→1, 0→1) and asserts both `shards.len()` and `shard_mask == shards - 1`; `default_cache_is_sized_16_by_4096_with_zero_counters` pins the exact Default constants; `debug_output_exposes_shape_and_live_counters` primes the cache with one miss + one hit and asserts the Debug string starts with `DecisionCache` and contains `shards: 4`, `capacity_per_shard: 8`, `hits: 1`, `misses: 1`; `clone_returns_empty_cache_with_same_sizing_and_zero_counters` exercises the source (miss+insert+hit → hits=1, misses=1), clones, and asserts the clone has no carried entry, hits=0 (but misses=1 from the lookup-that-proved-the-entry-was-gone), and the same shard count / per-shard capacity; `insert_dedup_does_not_overwrite_or_bump_fifo_position` is the strongest test — fills a 1-shard 2-cap cache with A then B, performs a dedup insert of A with a DIFFERENT outcome (Deny instead of Allow) to prove the existing Allow is not overwritten, then inserts C and asserts A was the one evicted (if the dedup had refreshed FIFO position, B would have been evicted instead); `cached_outcome_round_trip_preserves_rule_id_and_obligations` inserts a non-default outcome (`rule_id = Some("rule-42")`, `obligations = [MaskPoint, AuditExtended]`) and asserts every field survives the `CachedOutcome` round-trip including obligation ordering; `decision_key_distinct_secondary_hashes_do_not_collide` builds two keys with identical `primary`/`resource_hash`/`action`/`policy_epoch` but different `secondary` and asserts both `assert_ne!` equality AND cache-lookup miss-on-second — this is the 128-bit collision-resistance security claim made concrete; `decision_key_action_and_resource_are_part_of_identity` inserts a base key and performs cache lookups with the exact same subject but varied `action` (Send→Receive) and varied `resource_hash` (known→999), asserting both miss; `hits_and_misses_counters_track_independently` issues 2 misses followed by 3 hits and asserts `cache.hits() == 3 && cache.misses() == 2`; `shard_formula_consumes_secondary_hash_for_adversarial_resistance` pins the adversarial-resistance claim by building four keys that differ *only* in `secondary` (using `1u64 << {47, 48, 49, 50}` so `rotate_left(17)` places a non-zero low nibble) and asserting the resulting `shard_idx` set has more than one distinct element — if the formula ever drops `secondary`, every crafted key funnels into shard 0 and the assertion trips. Thirteen tests total (three existing + ten new), zero changes to any production API. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-nats::jetstream` `Checkpoint` load/save defensive branches and `JetStreamReplay` constructor field-preservation contract** — `crates/rustgate-nats/src/jetstream.rs` hosts two types that together own the situational-awareness bootstrap path every rustgate NATS consumer depends on: `Checkpoint` persists the last-processed JetStream stream sequence number to disk via `tokio::fs::read_to_string` / `write`, and `JetStreamReplay` drains the `TRACKS_HISTORY` stream on startup and populates `Router`'s `PositionCache` so newly connected TAK clients receive a full SA picture even when live publish traffic has not yet arrived. Before this PR the inline suite (3 tests: happy-path round-trip, invalid-content → `None`, missing-file → `None`) left every defensive branch of `Checkpoint::load` / `save` unpinned — a silent regression where `"42\n".parse::<u64>()` stopped accepting trailing whitespace (the file would otherwise be written by `echo 42 > checkpoint` during incident-response), where an empty/torn-write sentinel byte produced `Some(0)` instead of `None` (replaying the entire stream on every restart), where a pathological overflow value silently wrapped to `0`, where `-1` silently parsed instead of rejecting cleanly, where `save()` failed to create missing parent directories recursively (the checkpoint path is operator-supplied and often points into a fresh state directory), where `save()` on a bare filename with no parent panicked on the `Option::unwrap` hidden inside `Path::parent`, where `save()` appended rather than atomically overwrote (accumulating stale sequences forever and defeating the whole point of checkpointing), or where `u64::MAX` failed to round-trip could all ship green. Twelve new inline tests close the gap: `checkpoint_load_trims_whitespace_and_newlines_from_seq_string` pins the `.trim().parse::<u64>()` chain that the hand-edit workflow depends on; `checkpoint_load_empty_file_returns_none` pins the "empty file must parse as None, not Some(0)" invariant; `checkpoint_load_overflow_seq_returns_none` writes a 29-digit value and asserts the `.ok()` on the parse result routes cleanly to `None`; `checkpoint_load_negative_value_returns_none` pins that `u64::from_str` rejects leading `-` so a config migration that writes a signed `i64` representation by mistake fails loudly at load; `checkpoint_save_creates_parent_dirs_recursively` builds a four-level nested path (`a/b/c/d/checkpoint`) underneath a `tempdir` and asserts both the `save().await.unwrap()` call succeeds and the resulting `load().await` returns the written value, pinning the `tokio::fs::create_dir_all` branch every operator-supplied path relies on; `checkpoint_save_at_bare_filename_does_not_panic_on_none_parent` points `Checkpoint::new` at a bare filename inside `tempdir`-cwd (no directory component in the path), drives `save()` through the `Path::parent() == Some("")` branch that the naive `create_dir_all(path.parent().unwrap())` spelling would panic on, and asserts both success and round-trip — the test relies on `tempfile::tempdir`'s automatic cleanup + `std::env::set_current_dir` restoration via the `Drop` on the guard so parallel tests stay isolated; `checkpoint_save_is_atomic_overwrite_not_append` writes `42` then `7` and asserts the final file contents are exactly `"7"` (not `"427"` and not `"42\n7"`), pinning the `write()`-is-truncate contract that every subsequent `load()` relies on; `checkpoint_save_and_load_round_trips_u64_max` pins the `u64::MAX = 18_446_744_073_709_551_615` boundary so a future refactor that accidentally narrowed the on-disk encoding to `i64` (flipping the MSB and producing `-1` on reload) fails here rather than at a live operator incident; `checkpoint_debug_impl_renders_path_field` pins the `#[derive(Debug)]` output contains the `path: "..."` field so `tracing::debug!(?checkpoint, …)` lines stay human-readable and operators can grep for the configured checkpoint location at startup; `jetstream_replay_and_checkpoint_compile_time_debug_bounds` uses `fn assert_debug<T: std::fmt::Debug + ?Sized>()` parameterised on `Checkpoint` and `JetStreamReplay` to pin the `missing_debug_implementations = "warn"` workspace-lint compliance at test-compile time — a refactor that removed the derive would fail here rather than at a downstream `tracing::debug!` call site; `jetstream_replay_new_preserves_none_checkpoint_path` and `jetstream_replay_new_preserves_some_checkpoint_path` are the two `require_nats!`-gated tests that exercise `JetStreamReplay::new(client, NatsConfig::default(), checkpoint_path)` with both `None` and `Some(PathBuf)` arguments, format the result with `{replay:?}`, and assert the `checkpoint_path: None` / `checkpoint_path: Some("...")` field survives constructor verbatim — a silent regression where `new()` dropped the checkpoint argument on the floor would otherwise only surface after a restart fails to resume from the persisted sequence. Both `require_nats!`-gated tests skip cleanly when nats-server is absent from PATH (same `rustgate-test-utils::require_nats!` macro used by every other NATS-touching integration test in the workspace). Total suite grows from 3 to 15 tests; zero changes to any production API — every test drives the module's public/crate-private surface through its existing constructors and methods. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-federation::discovery::consul` catalog URL, serde contract, peer defaults, Debug redaction, and timeout constant** — `crates/rustgate-federation/src/discovery/consul.rs` is the Consul catalog backend for dynamic federation peer discovery (`/v1/catalog/service/<name>` HTTP polling → `Vec<PeerConfig>`, gated on the `peer-discovery-consul` feature). Before this PR the inline suite pinned four behaviours — trailing-slash stripping in `catalog_url`, `ServiceAddress` preferred over node `Address`, empty-string `ServiceAddress` falling back to node `Address`, and IPv6 bracketing — but left seven contract-shaped invariants unpinned, each of which could silently regress without tripping a live-Consul integration test. Nine new inline tests close the gaps: `catalog_url_without_trailing_slash_uses_single_separator` pins the no-trailing-slash branch of `trim_end_matches` so a regression that always appended `/` would produce `http://consul:8500//v1/...` and fail here (the doc-comment explicitly advertises "without a trailing slash" as the canonical form); `catalog_entry_deserializes_pascal_case_wire_fields` deserializes a real-shaped JSON payload with `ServiceID`, `ServiceAddress`, `ServicePort`, `Address` and asserts all four fields round-trip correctly — pins both `#[serde(rename_all = "PascalCase")]` and the explicit `#[serde(rename = "ServiceID")]` override (Consul emits the id field in all-caps specifically, and a dropped rename would still round-trip internally but fail on real Consul output); `catalog_entry_service_address_is_optional` omits the `ServiceAddress` key entirely from the JSON payload to pin the `Option<String>` tolerance — Consul omits this field when a service registered without an explicit address (common with Consul Connect / sidecar registrations) and an accidental narrowing to `String` would reject every such node; `entry_to_peer_applies_documented_defaults` drives a single entry through the mapper and asserts all twelve output fields against the hardcoded constants — `groups = ["__ANON__"]`, `inbound_groups = None`, `outbound_groups = None`, `enabled = true`, `is_outbound = true`, `trust_cert_cn = None`, `routing_mode = RoutingMode::default()`, `protocol_version = 2`, `jwt_token = None`, `region = None`, `zone = None`, `priority = 100` — so a silent flip (e.g. `enabled = false` during a "disable peers by default until review" refactor or a bump of `protocol_version` to 3 that breaks mixed-version fleets) fails a focused test rather than surfacing as a production federation outage; `entry_to_peer_already_bracketed_ipv6_is_not_double_bracketed` pins the `!host.starts_with('[')` guard so a `[2001:db8::1]` service address (the form the Consul HTTP API sometimes returns when a node registered with an explicit literal) does not become `[[2001:db8::1]]:9000` and fail every downstream `SocketAddr::parse`; `backend_name_is_stable_consul_token` resolves the `PeerDiscovery` trait's `backend_name()` through `&dyn PeerDiscovery` and asserts the `&'static str` return is exactly `"consul"` — operators grep for this token in reconciler log lines and a rename would break every deployed alerting rule; `debug_impl_redacts_http_client_and_renders_key_fields` pins the manual `Debug` impl's contract — `base_url` and `service` are visible for operators (a `tracing::debug!(?backend, …)` line should let the on-call identify which backend errored) but the `reqwest::Client` is elided via `finish_non_exhaustive()` and the rendered output must contain `..` (the sentinel) and must not contain `Client {` (which would indicate a silent swap to `#[derive(Debug)]` that would leak connector pool state into logs); `default_http_timeout_is_ten_seconds` pins both `DEFAULT_HTTP_TIMEOUT == 10s` and the critical arithmetic `DEFAULT_HTTP_TIMEOUT < 30s` (the reconciler's default interval) so a future bump past the reconciler tick would fail here instead of silently starving the discovery loop on every slow Consul response; `new_accepts_owned_string_and_str_slice` drives both `&str` and `String` call shapes through `ConsulDiscovery::new` to pin `impl Into<String>` compiles for both forms so a future narrowing to `&str` (which would break every owned-`String` caller in the server bin) fails at test-compile time rather than at the call site. Coverage grows from 4 to 13 tests; zero changes to production code. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-core::net` tactical-socket helpers (constants, SO_KEEPALIVE observability, idempotence, AsFd bound width)** — `crates/rustgate-core/src/net.rs` hosts the socket-hardening primitives (`configure_tcp_keepalive`, `configure_tcp_nodelay`, `configure_tactical_socket`) that every accepted TCP stream in the workspace flows through: TLS listener, plaintext TCP, WebSocket, MQTT, enrollment server, metrics server, federation connector, and the client dial side. Before this PR the inline suite (3 tests) only asserted `TCP_NODELAY` is observable after `configure_tcp_nodelay` and that the combined helper does not error, leaving five contract-shaped invariants unpinned. Six new tests close the gap: `keepalive_idle_and_interval_constants_pin_rustgate_defaults` pins `TCP_KEEPALIVE_IDLE = 30s` and `TCP_KEEPALIVE_INTERVAL = 10s` so an accidental edit to the top-of-file durations fails here rather than silently shifting every production listener's dead-connection detection curve — doubling the idle would push dead-peer cleanup past the `FEDERATION_HEARTBEAT_TIMEOUT` budget; `tcp_nodelay_starts_disabled_so_configure_is_not_a_no_op` is the defensive vacuity-guard — if a future OS ever ships sockets with Nagle already disabled, every nodelay assertion in this file would become vacuously true, so this test fails loudly in that case and forces a redesign of the positive-path tests rather than letting them pass silently; `configure_tcp_keepalive_enables_so_keepalive_flag` uses `SockRef::keepalive()` (cross-platform accessor) to pin that SO_KEEPALIVE is actually set — closing the "the call didn't error" gap the existing `keepalive_does_not_fail_on_tcp_listener` test leaves; `tactical_socket_enables_both_so_keepalive_and_tcp_nodelay_observably` is the end-to-end pin on the combined helper — the F-151 / #745 regression was that listeners set keepalive but forgot `TCP_NODELAY`, paying a ~40ms Nagle+DELACK penalty per tactical message, and `configure_tactical_socket` exists precisely to make the "keepalive + nodelay" pair atomic at every call site, so both observables must be asserted after a single call; `tactical_socket_is_idempotent_on_repeated_calls` pins the contract that the helpers are safe to call multiple times against the same live socket — shared code paths (session spawns, cert reloads, reconnection loops) can plausibly run more than once without a "has it been configured" gate, so toggling-on-second-call would be a regression; `tactical_socket_accepts_both_tcp_listener_and_tcp_stream_via_as_fd_bound` is the load-bearing compile-time + runtime self-check that the `impl AsFd` generic bound stays wide enough for every call site in the workspace — if a future refactor narrowed the bound to `&TcpStream`, the callers that pass a `TcpListener` (`server/metrics.rs`, `enrollment/server.rs`) would fail to compile, but not via this module's own suite. Suite grows from 3 to 9 tests; no behavioural change to the module under test. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-enrollment::contacts` Marti API provider (serde rename contract, Debug redaction, factory wrapper, zero-TTL edge case, double-check concurrency)** — `crates/rustgate-enrollment/src/contacts.rs` hosts the Marti-compatible `/Marti/api/contacts/all` and `/Marti/api/clientEndPoints` provider stack (`ContactInfo` struct, `ContactsProvider` callback alias, `CachedContactsProvider` with TTL cache, and the `cached_contacts_provider` free-function wrapper). Before this PR the inline suite only covered the happy-path cache TTL behaviour (three tests: within-ttl cache hit, post-ttl refresh, and post-ttl updated-data round-trip), leaving five contract-shaped invariants unpinned that a refactor could silently break without tripping the integration layer. Six new inline tests close that gap: `contact_info_serializes_with_marti_compatible_last_status_field` asserts the `#[serde(rename = "lastStatus")]` attribute is load-bearing for iTAK/ATAK wire-compat (a regression that dropped the rename would still round-trip between rustgate binaries but break every Marti consumer — the test pins both `obj.contains_key("lastStatus")` **and** `!obj.contains_key("last_status")` so the absence of the snake_case key is part of the contract); `contact_info_clone_and_partial_eq_round_trip` pins the `#[derive(Clone, PartialEq)]` derives that the cache relies on (`contacts.clone()` on every cache hit) so a future narrowing fails here rather than at transitive callers; `cached_contacts_provider_debug_shows_ttl_but_not_inner` pins the custom `Debug` impl's `finish_non_exhaustive()` call — the `..` marker in the rendered output is the contract that keeps the `Arc<dyn Fn>` callback pointer out of `tracing::debug!(?c, …)` lines while still exposing the `ttl` field operators want to see, and the test asserts both the presence of `ttl` + `..` and the absence of `inner` / `cache` tokens; `cached_contacts_provider_factory_function_wraps_with_ttl` drives the `cached_contacts_provider(inner, ttl) -> ContactsProvider` free-function wrapper that the server binary actually uses (the existing suite only exercised `CachedContactsProvider::new` directly, so a regression in the wrapper closure that accidentally skipped the cache and always called the inner would slip through) and asserts `call_count == 1` across two `wrapped().await` calls within the TTL; `zero_ttl_always_invokes_inner_provider` pins the ttl=0 edge case where `elapsed() < Duration::ZERO` is mathematically never true and both the read-lock fast path and the write-lock double-check must therefore fall through to inner on every call (operators who want "cache disabled" semantics rely on this — a regression that treated 0 as a sentinel for "no expiry" would silently pin the first snapshot forever); `concurrent_gets_on_fresh_cache_invoke_inner_exactly_once` uses `tokio::join!` with a 20ms sleep inside the inner callback to deliberately force the second caller onto the write-lock double-check branch and pins `call_count == 1` as the contract the fast path depends on to avoid a thundering-herd fan-out into the router's session state under a spike of concurrent Marti requests. Total suite grows from 3 to 9 tests; no behavioural change to the module under test. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-client::connect` dial-path defensive branches and plaintext `Stream::Plain` I/O** — `crates/rustgate-client/src/connect.rs` is the sole dial helper every integration test (and every downstream consumer of the lightweight client library) reaches through, and its existing inline suite covered seven sunny-path and TLS-configuration-error cases but left three defensive branches of `build_tls_config` / `dial_inner` unpinned and the entire `Stream::Plain` `AsyncRead`/`AsyncWrite`/`poll_shutdown` impl untouched by the unit-test layer. A silent refactor that (a) swallowed a `std::fs::read_to_string` error on the key path into a non-`Io` variant, (b) did the same on the CA path, (c) narrowed the `dial_inner` `MissingField("tls")` guard to only match on a public-API caller (so direct `ClientConfig` construction with `plaintext=false, tls=None` silently produced a `Stream::Plain` rather than the explicit error the field name promises), or (d) regressed any of the four `AsyncRead`/`AsyncWrite` forwarders on `Stream::Plain` (the two `poll_read`/`poll_write` vtable entries, `poll_flush`, `poll_shutdown`) could all ship green. Four new inline tests close those gaps: `build_tls_config_missing_key_file_returns_io_error` issues a real cert via `TestCa` but points the `key` field at a nonexistent path and asserts `Err(ClientError::Io(e))` with `e.kind() == ErrorKind::NotFound`, mirroring the existing cert-path test; `build_tls_config_missing_ca_file_returns_io_error` repeats the pattern on the CA path (the `ca` field is the last PEM the helper reads, and the matching error arm was previously uncovered); `dial_without_tls_on_non_plaintext_config_returns_missing_field` direct-constructs a `ClientConfig` with `plaintext=false, tls=None` (bypassing the `ClientConfigBuilder` which enforces invariants, to pin the defensive branch that exists specifically for callers who assemble `ClientConfig` by hand), spawns a throwaway `TcpListener` on `127.0.0.1:0` so `TcpStream::connect` succeeds and the test reaches the TLS-vs-plaintext switch in `dial_inner`, and asserts `ClientError::MissingField("tls")` — split into `Err(MissingField(field))` / `Err(other)` / `Ok(_)` arms because `Stream` does not derive `Debug` and the usual `{other:?}` panic formatter will not compile; `dial_plaintext_connects_and_stream_round_trips_bytes` spawns a local TCP echo, dials with `plaintext=true`, round-trips `b"ping"` to exercise `Stream::Plain`'s `poll_read`/`poll_write`/`poll_flush` vtable entries, then calls `stream.shutdown().await.unwrap()` to exercise `poll_shutdown` — the four delegations to the inner `TcpStream` are now covered by a single end-to-end test rather than relying on an integration-test side-effect. `cargo nextest run -p rustgate-client` reports 38/38 passing. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for the `rustgate-core::codec::Codec` trait contract** — `crates/rustgate-core/src/codec.rs` defines the `Codec` trait that every transport-layer codec (`TakXmlCodec`, `RawLineCodec`, `RawLengthPrefixedCodec`, `TakFrameCodec`, `MavlinkCodec`, `NoopCodec`) implements; the trait is the seam the router reaches for to go from wire bytes to a `RoutableMessage` and back. Before this PR the file had zero inline tests — the individual codec impls were covered by their own modules but the trait itself, its super-trait bounds (`Send + Sync + std::fmt::Debug`), its object-safety, and its method signatures had no direct test that would fail if a future refactor silently tightened one of the bounds to `Send + Sync` without `Debug` (breaking `tracing::debug!(?codec, …)` at every call site) or narrowed `decode_payload` from `BytesMut` to `&[u8]` (breaking the zero-copy `frame.freeze()` contract every passthrough codec relies on). A fresh inline `mod tests` introduces a minimal `StubCodec` that implements every required method and then pins the contract end-to-end: `codec_trait_is_object_safe_and_send_sync_debug` calls a `fn assert_bounds<T: Send + Sync + std::fmt::Debug + ?Sized>()` helper parameterised on `dyn Codec` (a compile-time assertion that `dyn Codec` is object-safe and carries the documented super-traits — a refactor that removed `Send`, `Sync`, or `Debug` fails at test-compile time rather than at the first transitive call site) and then boxes the stub and re-wraps it as `Arc<dyn Codec>` to prove both pointer shapes are accepted; `codec_passthrough_decode_payload_is_zero_copy` exercises the `BytesMut → Bytes::freeze()` passthrough path the TAK-XML and raw codecs depend on; `codec_encode_payload_reads_message_payload` builds a real `RoutableMessage` (via the full `Identity::new(cn, fingerprint, groups)` + `Destination::Broadcast` + `Metadata { received_at, source_protocol, message_type: MessageType::Other(...), ttl }` struct-literal shape every production encoder sees) and round-trips the payload through `encode_payload`; `codec_detect_returns_bounded_confidence_score` pins the `detect(&[u8]) -> f32` method's `0.0..=1.0` contract plus the empty-slice and non-matching-prefix edge cases that protocol auto-detection callers lean on; `codec_framing_strategy_round_trips_through_trait` exercises every non-deprecated `FramingStrategy` variant (`LengthPrefixed { header_size: HeaderSize::Two }`, `XmlStream`, `LineDelimited`, `TakProtobuf`, `Mavlink`) via `Box<dyn Codec>` and asserts the `Debug` rendering round-trips unchanged (the variant has no `PartialEq` so a `Debug`-string equality is the stable surface tests can use without ahead-of-time `derive` churn); `codec_debug_output_contains_stub_marker` pins the `#[derive(Debug)]` output so `tracing::debug!(?codec, …)` remains human-readable. Six tests total, zero changes to any production API — the trait definition itself is untouched. (Refs #909)
- **Optional `key_expirations` metadata on the encrypted-archive `KeyFile` + admission gating on expired active keys** — `rustgate_core::encrypted::KeyFile` gains an additive, optional `key_expirations: BTreeMap<String, i64>` field (unix seconds since epoch, per key id) serialised with `#[serde(default, skip_serializing_if = "BTreeMap::is_empty")]` so pre-existing key files deserialize unchanged and re-serialised legacy rings produce byte-identical JSON (pinned by `legacy_key_file_without_expirations_field_deserializes_cleanly` and `key_expirations_is_omitted_from_serialization_when_empty`). The `deny_unknown_fields` guard on `KeyFile` is preserved so a typo like `"key_expiraton"` still fails loudly rather than silently losing the field. `KeyRing::from_bytes` now validates that every id in `key_expirations` is also present in `keys` — a config-file typo that references a non-existent id (e.g. `"k2-typo"` when only `"k1"` exists) would otherwise silently leave the real active key never-expiring, so the load is rejected with a `KeyFile` error naming the offending id (pinned by `key_expirations_referencing_unknown_id_is_rejected`). `KeyRing` gains two new accessors: `not_after(&self, id: &str) -> Option<i64>` exposes the configured expiration for any id (returns `None` for both unknown ids and keys with no expiration — callers that need to distinguish the two cases combine this with `KeyRing::key`), and `active_key_expired_at(&self, now_unix_seconds: i64) -> bool` answers the admission question without reading the system clock itself so tests can deterministically drive both sides of the boundary. The boundary semantics are **inclusive at the `not_after` instant** (`not_after <= now_unix_seconds` returns `true`) — operators schedule rotations precisely on the minute, so "the key is unusable at and after this instant" is the less-surprising reading than "unusable strictly after" (pinned by `active_key_expired_at_boundary_is_exclusive_at_future_and_inclusive_at_past`). `EncryptedWriter::create` is the single admission gate: it reads `SystemTime::now().duration_since(UNIX_EPOCH)` once, calls `active_key_expired_at`, and returns a new `EncryptError::ActiveKeyExpired { id, not_after }` variant if the active key's expiration has passed — **without opening the underlying file**, so the refused path leaves zero side effects on disk (pinned by `encrypted_writer_create_refuses_when_active_key_is_expired` which asserts `!enc_path.exists()` on the failure branch). The existing `KeyRing::active_key()` accessor is deliberately **not** gated — historical data encrypted under an expired key must remain readable, so decryption of archives whose header references an expired key id still works; only the new-write admission path is blocked. Similarly, a non-active key's expiration is tracked for operator visibility but does not affect writes under a different active key (pinned by `non_active_key_expiration_does_not_block_active_key_writes`). The `Debug` impl on `KeyRing` additionally reports `expiring_key_count` alongside the existing `active_key_id` + `key_count` fields so a `tracing::debug!(?ring, …)` line surfaces the expiration configuration without leaking any actual unix-seconds values. Ten new tests in a `mod key_expiration` submodule cover: legacy load, parsed expirations accessor surface, unknown-id rejection, boundary inclusivity, no-expiration invariant, expired-active-key refusal with zero file side effects, future-expiration success path, non-active-key expiration harmlessness, serde round-trip with a non-empty map, and serde omission when the map is empty. Three `KeyFile { … }` constructions in `rustgate-server::archive`'s tests gain `key_expirations: BTreeMap::new()` for the new struct field. No behavioural change for key files that do not opt into expirations — the field is purely additive. (Refs #883)
- **`max_deliver` knob on the NATS JetStream pull consumer — caps poison-message redelivery loops** — `rustgate_nats::NatsConfig` gains a new `max_deliver: i64` field (default `0` = JetStream server default, unlimited; positive = per-message delivery cap) that is threaded through `subscriber::build_consumer_config` directly into `jetstream::consumer::pull::Config { max_deliver, .. }`. The default preserves existing behaviour — no observable change for operators who do not set it. Motivation: without a cap, a message that consistently fails between receive and ack (handler panic, task cancellation mid-route, transient router-side error that never drains) redelivers indefinitely, so a single poison message can saturate a subject's in-flight budget (`max_ack_pending`) and starve healthy traffic. Operators now get a defence-in-depth knob that JetStream enforces server-side: once a message exceeds `max_deliver` attempts, JetStream stops redelivering and advances past it (optionally publishing `$JS.EVENT.ADVISORY.CONSUMER.MAX_DELIVERIES.*.*` — opt-in for operators who want a SIEM signal on the drop). The wire-level `0` sentinel maps to JetStream's "use server default (unlimited)" semantics, so the field is safe to add without a config-file migration. New `--nats-max-deliver` CLI flag and `RUSTGATE_NATS_MAX_DELIVER` env var on `rustgate-server` (wired through the `define_config_file!` TOML overlay alongside the existing `nats_max_ack_pending`, `nats_ack_wait_secs`, and peer-list knobs), plus a `max_deliver` structured field on the `consumer configured` tracing line so operators can verify the effective value at startup. Covered by six new unit tests: three in `rustgate-nats::config` (`default_max_deliver_is_unlimited_sentinel`, `custom_max_deliver_round_trips`, `max_deliver_does_not_affect_validation`) and three in `rustgate-nats::subscriber` (`build_consumer_config_carries_max_deliver_from_nats_config`, `build_consumer_config_default_max_deliver_is_server_default_sentinel`, `build_consumer_config_high_max_deliver_round_trips`) that pin the projection from `NatsConfig` to `pull::Config` without needing a live NATS server — the consumer-config assembly is extracted as a free function `build_consumer_config(&NatsConfig) -> (String, pull::Config)` so it is unit-testable in isolation. Recommended starting value for production: `5`. (Refs #883)
- **Monotonic `seq` field on every hash-chained audit record for SIEM gap detection** — `rustgate_core::audit_chain::HashChainWriter` now injects a strictly-increasing `seq: u64` field into every record it writes, starting at `0` for the genesis record and resuming across restarts via a new `read_chain_state(path) -> io::Result<(tip, next_seq)>` helper that the server's main binary wires into the audit-log setup path. Before this PR the chain proved *ordering and integrity* (each record's `prev_sha256` links to the previous record's `sha256`, so any in-place edit invalidates every subsequent hash) but it did not prove *completeness*: an attacker who truncates N records from the tail and rewrites the `<path>.anchor` file produces a chain that still verifies under `verify_audit_log`, and a downstream SIEM tailing the log has no way to notice the missing window except by noting that events it was expecting to see never arrived. The sequence number closes that gap for any consumer that has ever seen a later `seq` — a 1→2→3 tail followed by a truncation to `1` is now detectable as "I previously observed seq=3, now I see seq=1" even if the chain hashes are all internally consistent (because the attacker rebuilt them). Design decisions: (a) `seq` is part of the canonical JSON that feeds `sha256`, so tampering with the counter invalidates the chain the same way tampering with any other field does — the new `tampering_with_injected_seq_is_detected_by_hash_chain` test pins this by flipping `seq:1` to `seq:42` on the middle record and asserting `VerifyError::HashMismatch { line: 2, .. }`; (b) the writer *overwrites* any caller-supplied `seq` field in the pre-chain record so downstream consumers can trust the counter regardless of what the tracing subscriber upstream emitted — a belt-and-suspenders defence against a future caller accidentally serialising a struct field named `seq`, pinned by `writer_overwrites_caller_supplied_seq`; (c) the saturating `next_seq = self.next_seq.saturating_add(1)` means a pathological operator who somehow reaches 2^64 records stalls the counter at `u64::MAX` instead of wrapping silently back to 0 — at 1M records/sec the ceiling is ~580,000 years out, but explicit saturation is cheaper than reasoning about wrap-around attacks against any downstream gap detector; (d) legacy audit logs written by pre-`seq` builds verify unchanged, because existing records already get past `verify_audit_log` without a `seq` field (nothing in the verifier enforces its presence), and on resume from such a log `read_chain_state` returns `next_seq = 0` so forward records restart the series cleanly — the `read_chain_state_reports_next_seq_zero_for_legacy_records` test hand-crafts a valid pre-`seq` log via the old `hash(prev || canonical_without_chain_fields)` recipe, proves the chain as a whole still verifies, and asserts the new writer resumes at `seq=0`. Recovery reuses `read_chain_tip`'s torn-write handling (bounded `REWIND_BUDGET` tail read, in-place truncation of corrupted trailing records) to avoid a second pass over a multi-GiB log: after `read_chain_tip` returns a clean tip, `read_chain_state` re-opens the file, seeks to `file_len - REWIND_BUDGET`, drops the partial leading line, strips the trailing newline, and parses `seq` out of the last record — if the last line no longer parses cleanly (i.e. the file changed between the two calls) we surface the race as `io::ErrorKind::InvalidData` instead of papering over it. `HashChainWriter::new(file, prev)` is unchanged (next_seq defaults to 0); a new builder method `with_next_seq(self, u64) -> Self` mirrors the existing `with_tip_observer` pattern so the server's audit setup path reads `(tip, next_seq) = read_chain_state(path)` and chains `HashChainWriter::new(file, tip).with_next_seq(next_seq).with_tip_observer(...)` in one expression. The public accessor `next_seq(&self) -> u64` exposes the cursor for tests and tooling that wants to snapshot writer state without flushing a record. Six new unit tests pin the contract: `writer_injects_monotonic_seq_from_genesis` (0,1,2 sequence on a fresh log), `writer_overwrites_caller_supplied_seq` (caller's `seq:9999` replaced with chain-owned `0`), `read_chain_state_resumes_seq_across_restart` (simulates restart, writes records 0-2, reopens with `next_seq=3`, appends 3-4, verifies full 0-4 sequence), `read_chain_state_returns_genesis_on_missing_file` (fresh install path returns `(GENESIS_PREV, 0)`), `read_chain_state_reports_next_seq_zero_for_legacy_records` (pre-seq log with hand-computed hashes returns `next_seq=0`), and `tampering_with_injected_seq_is_detected_by_hash_chain` (post-hoc seq edit triggers `HashMismatch`). All 22 pre-existing audit_chain tests continue to pass unchanged — `seq` is additive at the JSON level, the hash recipe is identical (prev_bytes || canonical_json, where canonical_json happens to now include `seq`), and `verify_audit_log` walks records by hash regardless of which fields they carry. (Refs #883)
- **Release-workflow archive-name guard — per-cell + cross-cell collision detection** — `.github/workflows/release-binary.yml` gains two defense-in-depth assertions so a future refactor of the packaging step cannot silently co-package FIPS and non-FIPS binaries under the same filename. The release upload uses `gh release upload ... --clobber`, so any two matrix cells producing the same archive path would silently overwrite each other — downstream consumers trust the filename token (`rustgate-fips-…`, `rustgate-pq-…`, `rustgate-…`) to drive FIPS policy, and a mis-named FIPS build shipped under the non-FIPS name would violate the NIST validation envelope that `aws-lc-fips-sys` pins. The per-cell check runs directly after `Package binary` and asserts the computed `$ARCHIVE` starts with the expected prefix derived from `matrix.fips`/`matrix.pq` (`true false` → `rustgate-fips-`, `false true` → `rustgate-pq-`, `false false` → plain `rustgate-` with an extra guard that the plain-default bucket does not accidentally carry either marker), failing loudly with a `::error::` annotation pointing at the mismatch; the cross-cell check runs in the downstream `combine-hashes` job after `download-artifact` merges every `.sha256` file into the workspace, extracts the archive column from every hash line with `awk '{print $2}' ./*.sha256`, sorts and `uniq -d`s the result, and fails if any duplicate remains. Both steps use `set -euo pipefail` so a bad shell quoting change surfaces immediately rather than silently returning zero. Scope is deliberately narrow — no change to the packaging logic itself, only new verification steps — so the build matrix, artefact names, and release tagging contract stay byte-identical; the only observable difference on a healthy release run is two `OK:` log lines. (Refs #887 F-21)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-transport::pem` re-export facade** — `crates/rustgate-transport/src/pem.rs` is a 13-line backward-compatibility shim (`pub use rustgate_core::pem::{parse_ca_store, parse_certs, parse_key};`) that `rustgate-client`, plus every transport integration test that reaches for the older import path, still depends on. Before this PR the file had zero inline tests, so a silent upstream rename in `rustgate-core::pem` (or an accidental removal from the `pub use` list during a cleanup pass) would only surface at a transitive call site — long after the offending commit. Three new inline tests close the gap by routing a `TestCa`-issued client cert and key through the facade path (`use super::{parse_ca_store, parse_certs, parse_key};`) and asserting each helper resolves correctly: `facade_parses_ca_cert_pem` pins `parse_certs` on the CA PEM, `facade_parses_client_cert_and_key` pins both `parse_certs` on the leaf PEM and `parse_key` on the PKCS#8 key PEM (matching the shape rcgen emits), and `facade_builds_ca_store` pins `parse_ca_store` produces a single-entry `RootCertStore`. The tests deliberately stay thin — the exhaustive parsing behaviour (empty PEM, malformed PEM, non-CA leaf, garbage DER) lives in `rustgate_core::pem`'s inline suite, so duplicating it here would be churn; this module's job is solely to pin the re-export surface so a break in the facade wiring fails CI on the first build. Closes the `transport/pem.rs` line item on #909. (Refs #909)
- **Audit event `store_forward.drain_panic` on reconnect-time offline-queue drain panics** — `rustgate_transport::Router::drain_offline_messages` runs the redb-backed `ClientQueue::drain` inside `tokio::task::spawn_blocking` (redb operations are synchronous and would otherwise stall the tokio runtime for large drains), and its three match arms cover the sunny path, the storage-error path (which already emits `warn!` with the structured error), and the `Err(JoinError)` path. The join-error path previously emitted only an operator-facing `warn!` and silently returned `Vec::new()` — a panic inside the blocking closure (a redb assertion, an allocator failure, a bug in a future version of the crate, or any future synchronous helper the closure grows) would therefore cost the connecting client their entire offline queue without producing any durable, SIEM-visible signal. Ephemeral warn lines do not survive log rotation, and a compliance trail that claims "all store-forward drops are audited" is weakened by a silent branch that is deliberately reachable via runtime panics. A new `rustgate_core::audit::store_forward_drain_panic(session_hash, error)` helper emits the canonical audit event (`event_type = "store_forward.drain_panic"`, `action = "drain_queue"`, `result = "panic"`, plus `session` — the PII-safe `hash_cn(cn)` — and `error` — the `JoinError::Display` rendering, which tokio formats as `task N panicked with message "..."`). The router's join-error arm now hashes the CN once, formats the error string once, preserves the existing `warn!` (operator log stream) for diagnostic continuity, and emits the audit event alongside so SIEM ingest and on-call dashboards receive the signal even when operational logs rotate aggressively. Pinned by two layers of tests: a smoke test in `rustgate-core::audit::tests::store_forward::test_store_forward_drain_panic` that matches the existing helper-invocation pattern, and a focused integration test in `crates/rustgate-transport/tests/store_forward_drain_panic_audit.rs` that (a) installs a `tracing_subscriber::fmt::layer().json()` filtered on `target = "audit"`, (b) forces a real `JoinError` by panicking inside `tokio::task::spawn_blocking`, (c) asserts `JoinError::is_panic()` classification to pin the contract meaning, (d) calls the audit helper with the actual `JoinError::Display` string, and (e) parses the captured JSON line and asserts every documented field (`target`, `level`, `event_type`, `session`, `action`, `result`, and the `error` field carries the `"panic"` token so the human-readable cause survives the serialisation round-trip). Stands up the defensive contract against a class of silent-loss regressions that a full-unit-test-the-internal-redb-panic approach cannot reach without an intrusive fault-injection abstraction. Closes #807.
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-mqtt::MqttServer::run` accept-loop lifecycle** — `crates/rustgate-mqtt/src/server.rs` is the MQTT transport's accept orchestrator: it destructures `MqttServerConfig`, spawns one `handle_mqtt_session` task per mTLS-authenticated connection, fires the `on_accept` / `on_reject` counter callbacks at the admission boundary, and `tokio::select!`s on `listener.accept()` versus `shutdown.notified()` to terminate cleanly on SIGTERM. Before this PR the file had zero inline tests — the integration suite at `crates/rustgate-mqtt/tests/mqtt_integration.rs` covered the full handshake + CONNECT + PUBLISH + SUBSCRIBE path end-to-end (117 tests) but none of them isolated the lifecycle invariants the accept loop itself owns, so a refactor that (a) accidentally dropped the `Debug` derive on `MqttServer`, (b) broke the shutdown-notify branch of the select (causing the server to hang on SIGTERM instead of draining), (c) cloned the `Arc<Notify>` during config destructure so external `stop_subsystem` callers could no longer trigger shutdown, or (d) introduced a panicking destructor path on the observer callbacks could all land without tripping a wire-level test. Seven new inline tests close that gap: `server_type_is_debug` and `server_debug_renders_struct_name` pin the `#[derive(Debug)]` contract so a future `tracing::debug!(?server, …)` call site stays compilable; `run_returns_immediately_when_shutdown_pre_notified` relies on `tokio::sync::Notify`'s one-permit-storage semantics to prove the select fires the shutdown branch on the first poll when the notify was armed before spawn; `run_returns_when_shutdown_fires_after_spawn` is the more realistic SIGTERM-during-idle path — spawn `run`, yield once to let it park on the select, then notify and bound the task's termination under a 5s `tokio::time::timeout`; `run_ignores_extra_shutdown_notifications` verifies that a second notify after the loop has already exited is a silent no-op (so operators can idempotently signal shutdown without re-arming the notify and stranding the next `run` invocation); `shutdown_notify_is_observed_by_concurrent_readers` clones the `Arc<Notify>` before moving it into config and fires from the external holder to pin that the run loop awaits the *same* handle the caller holds — a regression here would break `rustgate-server`'s `stop_subsystem` helper silently; `run_exits_cleanly_with_observers_installed` installs panicking `on_accept` / `on_reject` closures (which *must not fire* on a shutdown path with no inbound clients) and asserts clean termination, so a future refactor that accidentally fired the callbacks on the shutdown branch would panic the test. All seven tests are scoped to the `flavor = "current_thread"` runtime for deterministic yield behaviour and reuse the `TestCa` + `TlsConfig::from_pem` + `MqttListener::bind("127.0.0.1:0", ...)` pattern established by `crates/rustgate-mqtt/src/listener.rs`'s own inline suite, so no new dev-dependency is introduced. (Refs #909)
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-websocket::WebSocketServer::run` accept-loop lifecycle and callback type-alias contracts** — `crates/rustgate-websocket/src/server.rs` is the WebSocket transport's accept orchestrator and the home of five cross-thread callback type aliases (`OnNatsPublish`, `OnFederationForward`, `OnHttpForward`, `OnWsAcceptFn`, `OnWsRejectFn`) plus the `WsSessionHooks`, `WsServerConfig`, and `WebSocketServer` types. Before this PR the file had zero inline tests — the integration suite at `crates/rustgate-websocket/tests/ws_server.rs` (12 tests) covered the full handshake + Upgrade + framing path end-to-end but nothing isolated the invariants that never touch a socket: `#[derive(Debug)]` on `WebSocketServer`, `Send + Sync + 'static` bounds on every callback alias (a refactor that narrowed any of them to `FnMut` or dropped `Send` would only surface at a transitive call site in `rustgate-server::setup`), the manual `Debug` impls' `on_*: true`/`false` boolean-sentinel rendering for `WsSessionHooks` (which exists specifically so `tracing::debug!(?config, …)` does not leak a non-`Debug` `Arc<dyn Fn>` closure pointer), `WsServerConfig::Debug`'s redaction of the `Router` inner state and its rendering of the codec's `protocol_id` (which operators grep in startup logs), the pure async helper `tick_or_never(None)` which the write loop relies on to keep the Ping-timer select arm disarmed without ever completing, and the four shutdown-responsiveness paths of `WebSocketServer::run`'s `tokio::select!` (pre-notified shutdown, post-spawn notified shutdown, shutdown driven from a cloned external `Arc<Notify>` handle mirroring `stop_subsystem` callers, and shutdown with panicking `on_connection_accept` / `on_connection_rejected` observers installed to prove neither fires on the clean-shutdown-no-clients path). Nineteen new inline tests close that gap: three pin the `WebSocketServer` `Debug` surface and the `DEFAULT_MAX_WRITE_BUFFER_BYTES = 4 MiB` constant, five compile-time-assert the `Send + Sync + 'static` bounds on every callback alias via `fn assert_bounds<T: Send + Sync + 'static>(_: &T)`, four exercise `WsSessionHooks` (`default` all-None, `Debug` `false`-sentinel rendering, `Debug` `true`-sentinel rendering with both callbacks installed, `Clone` preserving `Arc` strong-count rather than wrapping in an outer `Arc`), one pins `WsServerConfig::Debug`'s `<Router>` redaction and `protocol_id` rendering, two drive `tick_or_never` (bounded non-resolution on `None` under a 50ms timeout, prompt resolution on `Some` using a 1ms `tokio::time::interval`), and four pin the shutdown-responsiveness matrix of `run`. A local stub `NoopCodec` satisfies the `Codec` trait (protocol_id / framing / decode_payload / encode_payload / detect) without dragging in the `rustgate-transport` codec stack, and a `build_config` helper assembles the 17-field `WsServerConfig` with the same `TestCa` + `TlsConfig::from_pem` + `WsListener::bind("127.0.0.1:0", ...)` pattern the existing integration tests use. No new dev-dependency is introduced. (Refs #909)
- **Per-transport `transport` field on MQTT and WebSocket session-lifecycle logs** — `crates/rustgate-mqtt/src/lib.rs` and `crates/rustgate-websocket/src/lib.rs` each export a new `pub const TRANSPORT_LABEL: &str` (`"mqtt"` and `"websocket"` respectively), and every session-lifecycle `info!`/`warn!` emission inside `server.rs` and `session.rs` in both crates now carries `transport = TRANSPORT_LABEL` as a structured field. The goal is parity with `rustgate-transport` and `rustgate-federation`, both of which already emit `transport = "…"` on startup, accept, reject, and shutdown lines — downstream log aggregators filter on this field to build per-transport dashboards and alerting rules, and without it the MQTT / WebSocket session lifecycle disappears into a generic "server started" / "client rejected" stream that cannot be sliced by protocol. Covered sites: server started, connection-limit rejected, accept error, server shutting down (MQTT × 4 in `server.rs`); session started, client-ID collision rejection, tenant-quota rejection, offline-message filter, session ended (MQTT × 5 in `session.rs`); the matching WebSocket seven (server started, connection-limit rejected, accept error, server shutting down, session started, offline-message filter, tenant-quota rejection, session ended). Exposed as a named constant rather than a string literal at every call site so a future rename surfaces as a single-point diff rather than a silent per-line drift, and so downstream log-parser grep patterns can key off the exact token the constant carries. No behavioural change to any control path — additive field on existing log events. (Refs #908)
- **Prometheus `rustgate_crl_age_seconds` gauge (feature `metrics`)** — surfaces the age in seconds of the stalest loaded CRL's `thisUpdate` timestamp (maximum elapsed time across every configured CRL PEM) so operators can alert on a publisher that has stopped refreshing its revocation list. The gauge is a single no-label time series, set at startup inside `rustgate_server::setup::build_tls_config` after `TlsConfig::with_crls(...)` succeeds (both the `--spiffe` and classical `--cert/--key/--ca` branches) and refreshed inside `reload_all_configs`'s SIGHUP-driven CRL re-parse path; the new `TlsConfig::crl_max_age_seconds(&self)` convenience method routes both paths through the same free function `rustgate_transport::config::crl_max_age_seconds(&[CertificateRevocationListDer])`, so a single helper owns the SHA-256-free "pick the largest `now - thisUpdate`" reduction. Individual parse failures are silently skipped rather than collapsing the signal to `None` — a single malformed sibling CRL must not erase the freshness signal from the healthy ones — but if every entry fails to re-parse the helper returns `None` and the gauge keeps its prior value (default `0` when never set). `TlsConfig::validate_crl_file(path)` now returns a new `CrlFileSummary { count, max_age_seconds }` struct (replacing the bare `Result<usize>`) so the SIGHUP path can publish the freshest age alongside the existing `count` audit field; the only external caller — `rustgate-server`'s `reload_crl_file` helper — is updated accordingly, and the new `crl_max_age_seconds` audit field surfaces the age on every successful reload so operators can eyeball "reload succeeded but the CRL is 48h old" in logs even before the Prometheus path is wired. Seven new unit tests pin the contract end-to-end: four in `rustgate-transport::config::tests::crl_age` (`empty_slice_returns_none`, `fresh_crl_reports_small_age` — asserts age ∈ [0, 60] seconds for a just-generated CRL with 60s of tolerated clock skew against `ASN1Time::now()`, `malformed_crl_bytes_suppress_entry_but_keep_siblings` — mixes a real CRL with a minimal bogus `0x30 0x00` SEQUENCE and confirms the real entry still anchors the age, and `all_unparseable_returns_none`), and three in `rustgate-server::metrics::tests` (`crl_age_gauge_registered_under_documented_name` pins the `rustgate_crl_age_seconds` family name, single time-series, and empty-label shape via `default_registry().gather()`, `crl_age_gauge_set_and_read_roundtrip` pins the idempotent set/get path without tripping parallel readiness tests, and `crl_age_gauge_fed_by_tlsconfig_helper_for_real_crl` runs the full production sequence `TestCa::issue_server_cert` → `TlsConfig::from_pem` → `TlsConfig::with_crls` → `TlsConfig::crl_max_age_seconds` → `crl_age_seconds().set(...)`, proving the setup.rs wiring path is exercised end-to-end). Feature-gated under `metrics`; absent that feature the gauge wiring compiles out via `#[cfg(feature = "metrics")]`. (Refs #908)
- **`rustgate_position_cache_size` Prometheus gauge** — the transport-layer `PositionCache` (latest-position-per-CN store that feeds replay to newly connected sessions) previously shipped dark: operators had no way to see how full it was, making it impossible to distinguish "capacity-driven oldest-entry eviction is happening right now" from "the cache is healthy and well below `DEFAULT_MAX_ENTRIES = 100_000`". The gap was called out in #908 alongside the equivalent dedup-cache gauge that landed earlier. A new `IntGauge` is registered in `rustgate_server::metrics` under the workspace's shared `OnceLock + init() + accessor + fallback-constructor` pattern and wired into the cache via a new `OnCacheSize = Arc<dyn Fn(usize) + Send + Sync>` callback hot-swapped through `ArcSwap<Option<OnCacheSize>>` on `&self` (so `main.rs` can install it after the router is constructed without needing `&mut` on a by-value field), keeping `rustgate-transport` metrics-agnostic — no `prometheus` dependency leaks into the transport crate. `PositionCache::store` / `remove` / `sweep` capture the post-mutation `entries.len()` inside the write-lock scope and fire the callback after the lock is released so hot-path work never blocks on gauge state; `sweep` only fires when at least one entry was removed. Six cache-layer unit tests pin the three mutation points, capacity-driven eviction (net size stays at `max_entries` after a replacement insert), callback replacement (old closure must not fire after `set_on_size` is called a second time), and the no-callback no-op path. Three server-layer tests cover registration under the documented metric name, set/read round-trip with prior-value restore so parallel tests stay isolated, and the full production wiring end-to-end (`PositionCache::new()` → `set_on_size` pointing at the gauge → `store`/`remove` round-trip → gauge value assertions). (#908)
- **`rustgate_build_info` Prometheus gauge for scrape-time fleet-version tracking** — `rustgate_server::metrics::init()` now registers a Prometheus `IntGaugeVec` named `rustgate_build_info` with three labels (`version`, `rust_version`, `features`) pinned at startup to `env!("CARGO_PKG_VERSION")`, `env!("CARGO_PKG_RUST_VERSION")`, and a comma-separated list of enabled optional features resolved at compile time via `cfg!(feature = "...")`. The metric carries exactly one time series per process and is pinned at value `1` for the lifetime of the process — this is the standard Prometheus `*_build_info` convention (matching `go_build_info`, `kube_pod_info`, etc.) so Grafana `group by` / `topk` over these labels is cardinality-safe and existing dashboards/alerting rules that key off the idiom can be reused without custom wiring. The previous deployment story relied on operators hitting the unauthenticated `/version` endpoint from a scraper script (or reading the binary's embedded version string out of logs); both approaches miss feature-flag information entirely, which is the load-bearing signal when debugging why a new capability is not live on a given node (wrong build variant shipped) versus why it is misbehaving (right build, bad config). The feature string always includes `metrics` first (the module itself is feature-gated, so reaching this code path proves the feature is on) and then enumerates every optional feature rustgate currently compiles: `nats`, `enrollment`, `est`, `proto`, `full-detail`, `federation`, `federation-ha`, `peer-discovery-dns-sd`, `peer-discovery-consul`, `peer-discovery-etcd`, `websocket`, `mqtt`, `otel`, `fips`, `post-quantum`, `jemalloc`, `ocsp`, `spiffe`, `cedar`, `http`. Each is emitted only when its cfg is active, so a minimal build produces `features="metrics"` and a full build produces the maximal comma-joined list. Five unit tests pin the contract: `build_info_labels_shape` asserts the documented label order so scrapers cannot silently break on a rename, `enabled_features_always_includes_metrics` guards the invariant that the function is only reachable inside a metrics-feature build, `build_info_registered_after_init` pins that `init()` populates the `OnceLock`, `build_info_exposes_pinned_label_tuple_at_one` exercises the `.with_label_values(...)` lookup with the real compile-time strings and asserts the gauge reads `1`, and `build_info_gathered_from_default_registry` walks `prometheus::default_registry().gather()` to confirm the metric is actually reachable on a scrape (label set matches `BUILD_INFO_LABELS` modulo Prometheus's alphabetical ordering, single time series, value 1). No runtime cost — the labels are computed once at startup and never touched again. (Refs #908)
- **MQTT PUBLISH / SUBSCRIBE operation counters** — four new Prometheus `IntCounter`s close the operation-level observability gap left by the connection-level counters: `rustgate_mqtt_publishes_total` fires once per PUBLISH packet after topic validation, prefix mapping, and group-membership authorisation all pass and the message has been handed to `Router::route`; `rustgate_mqtt_publishes_rejected_total` fires once per PUBLISH refused on the session-handler boundary (QoS2 unsupported, topic validation, prefix-mapping, authorisation); `rustgate_mqtt_subscribes_total` and `rustgate_mqtt_subscribes_rejected_total` fire **per filter** (a SUBSCRIBE carrying three filters where two are denied produces two reject and one accept increment), because the MQTT 5 SUBACK shape requires per-filter reason codes and an operator needs to see which filters in a batch were refused rather than averaging the outcome across the packet. Without these four, a dashboard showing `rustgate_mqtt_connections_total` climbing can't distinguish "clients connected but never published" (credential-scope mismatch on the producer) from "clients published but their topics were refused" (ACL misalignment) — both look like `publishes_total` staying flat, but the reject counter tells you which one. The wiring follows the same metrics-agnostic pattern as the connection-level pair: `rustgate-mqtt` gains four new public type aliases `OnMqttPublish`, `OnMqttPublishReject`, `OnMqttSubscribe`, `OnMqttSubscribeReject` (all `Arc<dyn Fn() + Send + Sync>`) bundled into a new `MqttSessionHooks` struct that `MqttServerConfig` carries as `session_hooks: Option<MqttSessionHooks>`. Bundling into a struct (rather than four independent `Option<...>` fields) keeps `handle_mqtt_session`'s argument count at 10 instead of 13 without masking the individual hooks at the fire site — the session handlers still pull the specific callback out with `let_chains`. `rustgate-mqtt` **does not** gain a `prometheus` dependency; the `rustgate-server` side wires the four `.inc()` closures under `#[cfg(feature = "metrics")]` and the metric accessors themselves are `#[cfg(feature = "mqtt")]`-gated so a metrics-on-but-mqtt-off release build (the `binary-size` and `check-features --feature-powerset` CI jobs) stays warning-free. Fire-point placement is inline at each early-return site in `handle_publish_v{4,5}` and `handle_subscribe_v{4,5}` rather than classifying a post-hoc `Result`, because a Result-based classifier would conflate business rejection (QoS2 / topic / authz) with infrastructure failure (`BrokenPipe` on `send_response`) and over-count. A helper-per-outcome (`fire_publish_accept` / `fire_publish_reject` / `fire_subscribe_accept` / `fire_subscribe_reject`) keeps the fire pattern uniform so a future handler addition can't forget to fire. Twenty new tests total: seven in `rustgate-mqtt::config` pin the type aliases' `Send + Sync` bounds (compile-time assertion so a refactor that narrowed to `FnMut` would fail test-compile rather than at a transitive caller), the `MqttSessionHooks::default()` all-None shape, the manual `Debug` impl's `Some(<cb>)` / `None` sentinels (callback trait objects do not derive `Debug`, so a `tracing::debug!(?config, …)` site otherwise can't render without leaking a closure pointer), and the `Clone` impl's `Arc`-duplication semantics; thirteen in `rustgate-server::metrics::tests::mqtt_publish_subscribe_counters` pin the counter lifecycle end-to-end (four `*_registered_under_documented_name` assertions confirm the counter name against `prometheus::default_registry().gather()` and bind `.name()` — **not** the deprecated `.get_name()` — along with zero label cardinality, four `*_increments_are_observable` assert `.inc()` is monotonically visible via `> before` — **not** the `int_plus_one`-flagged `>= before + 1`, four `fed_by_mqtt_on_*_callback_end_to_end` tests round-trip the exact `Arc<dyn Fn()>` shape that `setup.rs` installs through the same `MqttSessionHooks`-resident dispatch the session task uses so a silent refactor of either the callback alias or the fire site would fail CI, and one `session_hooks_struct_accepts_all_four_counter_callbacks` builds the full struct end-to-end and drives all four closures so a cross-crate field-rename between `rustgate-mqtt` and `rustgate-server` setup.rs fails at test-compile time). Three existing MQTT integration-test fixtures in `mqtt_integration.rs` gain `session_hooks: None` to keep compilation green. (Refs #908)
- **MQTT listener connection accept/reject counters** — two new Prometheus `IntCounter`s expose the MQTT accept-loop's admission outcomes alongside the existing `rustgate_nats_publishes_total` family: `rustgate_mqtt_connections_total` fires once per accepted TCP connection (after the kernel-level accept succeeds and before the per-session spawn), and `rustgate_mqtt_connections_rejected_total` fires once per rejected connection (accept-time `TcpStream::from_std` failures, which are the only MQTT-listener-level admission failures — TLS handshake failures are scoped to `rustgate_tls_handshake_total` once that metric lands). Without these, operators have no single-signal way to distinguish "no MQTT traffic" from "the listener is healthy but every handshake is failing" — the two most common production causes (cert/CRL mismatch vs. genuine idle) are visually indistinguishable in `rustgate_connections_active` alone. The wire-up follows the same metrics-agnostic pattern the transport and federation crates already use: `rustgate-mqtt` gains two new public type aliases `OnMqttAccept = Arc<dyn Fn() + Send + Sync>` and `OnMqttReject = Arc<dyn Fn() + Send + Sync>` plus matching `on_accept: Option<OnMqttAccept>` and `on_reject: Option<OnMqttReject>` fields on `MqttServerConfig`; `rustgate-mqtt` crate **does not** gain a `prometheus` dependency — the transport crate stays protocol-layer-only, and `rustgate-server` composes the callbacks under `#[cfg(feature = "metrics")]` so a metrics-off build drops the counter entirely rather than carrying a no-op `.inc()` call. Because `MqttServerConfig` now carries callback trait objects, `#[derive(Debug)]` is replaced with a manual `Debug` impl that renders the callback fields as `"Some(<cb>)"` or `"None"` sentinels — the same pattern `rustgate-transport` uses for its hot-swap callbacks — so a `tracing::debug!(?config, …)` on the startup path cannot leak a caller-owned closure pointer into logs. The fire-points in `MqttServer::run` are placed at the exact admission boundary: accept callback fires after the `TcpStream::from_std` succeeds but before the `session::spawn` for the connection, and reject callback fires on the same-arm `Err(_)` branch alongside the existing `audit::connection_rejected` emission so the counter and the audit log cannot drift. Ten new tests total: four in `rustgate-mqtt` pin the type aliases (`on_accept_type_is_send_sync_arc_fn`, `on_reject_type_is_send_sync_arc_fn` use the compile-time `is_send_sync_arc_fn::<T>()` assertion helper so a refactor that dropped `Send + Sync` would fail at test-compile time rather than at some transitive caller's integration site) and the `Debug` impl (`debug_none_observers_render_as_none` and `debug_some_observers_render_as_cb_sentinel` wrap `MqttListener::bind` under `#[tokio::test]` because construction requires a live runtime for the underlying `TcpListener::bind` call — the non-async `#[test]` form panics with "there is no reactor running"); six in `rustgate-server` under `mod mqtt_connections_counters` pin the counter lifecycle end-to-end (registration with the default registry via `init()`, `.name()` and help-text assertions against `gather()`-returned `MetricFamily` instances using the new `prometheus::proto::MetricFamily::name()` accessor — **not** the deprecated `.get_name()` — increment-by-one semantics under `.inc()` using `> before` comparisons — **not** the `int_plus_one`-flagged `>= before + 1` form — and fallback-accessor behaviour when `init()` has not been called so a metrics-off-then-on dev loop does not panic). Three existing MQTT integration-test fixtures in `mqtt_integration.rs` gain `on_accept: None, on_reject: None` to keep compilation green. (Refs #908)
- **`rustgate_cert_expiry_seconds` Prometheus gauge for alertable server-cert rotation SLOs** — `rustgate_server::metrics::init()` now registers an `IntGauge` named `rustgate_cert_expiry_seconds` that reports `notAfter - now` in whole seconds for the active server leaf certificate (the one served on every mTLS handshake via the shared `CertReloader`). The value is refreshed as a side effect of `check_tls_cert`, which is called on every `/ready` dependency poll: operators who wire their load-balancer readiness probe to rustgate get a near-real-time expiry signal without a separate scraper. The value can go negative — the gauge *does not* clamp at zero — so an alert rule like `rustgate_cert_expiry_seconds < 604800` (7 days remaining) mirrors the existing `check_tls_cert` degradation threshold, and a second rule like `rustgate_cert_expiry_seconds < 0` pages on an already-expired cert that is still being served. Alerting only on the readiness endpoint's `degraded`/`unhealthy` verdict would miss the case where an operator has disabled the readiness probe or where the probe target does not scrape the JSON into an alerting backend. Three unit tests pin the contract: `cert_expiry_gauge_set_to_positive_remaining_seconds_for_valid_cert` issues a fresh test cert, calls `check_tls_cert`, and asserts the gauge reads positive; `cert_expiry_gauge_negative_after_expired_cert_check` uses `TestCa::issue_expired_client_cert` to drive the gauge negative and pin the "no clamp at zero" property that alert rules depend on; `cert_expiry_gauge_registered_under_documented_name` walks `prometheus::default_registry().gather()` to confirm the metric is reachable under its stable documented name (a rename would silently break every deployed alert rule). No runtime cost on the handshake hot path — the gauge is only touched during readiness evaluation. (Refs #908)
- **`rustgate_ws_connections_total` and `rustgate_ws_connections_rejected_total` Prometheus counters** — the WebSocket listener previously emitted no lifecycle telemetry, so a per-CN tenant-quota breach, a connection-rate-limiter trip, an origin/subprotocol rejection, or a global admission-control rejection disappeared silently while the only observable signal was the absence of a subsequent MQTT/CoT message. The new counters close the gap: `rustgate_ws_connections_total` increments once per fully-established WebSocket session (after the HTTP Upgrade succeeds, the per-IP rate limit passes, any Origin/Subprotocol/handshake-limit checks pass, the `WsListener::accept` semaphore admits, TLS completes if enabled, and the router-level `SessionHandle` registers successfully), and `rustgate_ws_connections_rejected_total` increments on every pre-session early-return branch across the two refusal paths — the accept-loop rejections in `WsListener::accept` (per-IP rate limit, semaphore try-acquire, TLS handshake failure, HTTP upgrade failure, origin/subprotocol reject) and the post-accept rejections in `handle_ws_session` (global-admission `BackpressureGate::try_acquire` fail, per-tenant-quota `register` fail). A new `WsSessionHooks { on_connection_accept, on_connection_rejected }` bundle carries the two `Arc<dyn Fn() + Send + Sync>` callbacks through `WsServerConfig` rather than adding two more bare fields — keeps the `handle_ws_session` signature bounded and matches the `On*` callback idiom already used across the workspace. `WsListenerConfig` gets its own `on_connection_rejected` slot so accept-loop refusals that never reach `handle_ws_session` (the five early returns in `accept()`) still increment; `setup.rs` clones the same `Arc` into both configs under `#[cfg(feature = "metrics")]` so the two refusal paths feed one counter. The transport crate stays metrics-agnostic — no `prometheus` dependency leaks into `rustgate-websocket`; the counters are registered in `rustgate-server::metrics` behind `#[cfg(all(feature = "metrics", feature = "websocket"))]` with the standard `OnceLock + init + accessor + fallback` pattern. Twelve new tests pin the contract: five in `rustgate-websocket::tests::session_hooks` (type-alias `Send + Sync + 'static` bounds, `Debug` redacts closure bodies, `Clone` shares callback state, struct default is all-None, `WsListenerConfig::default` has no reject hook), and seven in `rustgate-server::metrics::tests::ws_connection_counters` (counter registered under documented name × 2, increment observable × 2, full end-to-end callback plumbing × 2, session-hooks struct accepts both counter callbacks × 1). Firing sites explicitly verified: `lib.rs::accept` fires reject on the five early-return branches (rate-limit exceed, semaphore `try_acquire_owned` fail, TLS handshake error, HTTP upgrade error, origin/subprotocol reject); `server.rs::run` fires reject when connection-limit `try_acquire_owned` fails inline; `server.rs::handle_ws_session` fires reject when `BackpressureGate::try_acquire` returns `None` and when `router.register(...)` returns `false` (tenant quota exceeded), and fires accept on the success path immediately after `router.register` returns `true`. (Refs #908)
- **`rustgate_tls_handshake_duration_seconds` histogram — TLS handshake
  latency by outcome** — `crates/rustgate-transport/src/listener.rs` gains
  a new callback slot `OnHandshakeDuration = Arc<dyn Fn(Duration, &str) +
  Send + Sync>` wired through `TlsListener::set_on_handshake_duration`,
  alongside the existing `set_on_handshake_error` hook. The accept loop
  captures `Instant::now()` immediately before the
  `TlsAcceptor::accept(tcp_stream)` future and, on resolution, invokes
  the callback with `handshake_start.elapsed()` plus the outcome label
  (`"success"` when the TLS stream is produced, `"failure"` when the
  acceptor errors). The measurement boundary is deliberately the TLS
  layer itself: it excludes the upstream TCP accept and the downstream
  identity-extraction work so that operators can distinguish
  cryptographic-cost regressions (ring/aws-lc-rs / PQ key-exchange
  changes) from filesystem / CA-chain parsing latency. The observation
  fires on both branches so a handshake that fails fast still contributes
  a data point — without it, dashboards built on
  `histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(rustgate_tls_handshake_duration_seconds_bucket[5m]))`
  would under-count pathology. In `rustgate-server::metrics.rs` the new
  `tls_handshake_duration_seconds()` accessor exposes a registered
  `HistogramVec` with label `outcome` and a tactical bucket range
  `[0.001, 0.005, 0.01, 0.025, 0.05, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 1.0, 2.5, 5.0, 10.0]`
  — the 1 ms floor captures ring's best-case LAN handshakes (~2–5 ms)
  without collapsing the P50 into the first bucket; the 100–500 ms
  brackets cover cross-WAN paths and PQ/hybrid kx groups (ML-KEM-768
  adds ~30–60 ms per round trip on commodity CPUs); the 10 s ceiling
  catches pathological stalls before the TLS acceptor surrenders.
  Registration lives in `init()` and a new `fallback_histogram_vec`
  helper keeps the existing `Histogram`/`HistogramVec` fallback pattern
  symmetric with `fallback_counter_vec` so accessors stay safe when
  `init()` has not been called. In `rustgate-server::setup.rs` an
  additional `#[cfg(feature = "metrics")]` block installs the observer
  closure immediately after the handshake-error callback, mirroring the
  existing wiring style. Five new tests: `listener.rs` covers the
  setter's overwrite semantics (`set_on_handshake_duration_accepts_and_overwrites_callback`)
  and a compile-time `Send + Sync + 'static` bound on the type alias;
  `metrics.rs` covers canonical-name registration
  (`tls_handshake_duration_is_registered_under_canonical_name`), sum
  delta on observe (`tls_handshake_duration_success_label_observes`),
  label isolation between success / failure series
  (`tls_handshake_duration_failure_label_observes_separately_from_success`),
  and bucket coverage across the floor / ceiling
  (`tls_handshake_duration_bucket_floor_separates_from_ceiling`). Refs #908.
- **Unit-test coverage for `rustgate-server::setup` CLI→subsystem helpers** — `crates/rustgate-server/src/setup.rs` wires every subsystem the server starts (listener, authz, federation, enrollment, NATS, HTTP, UDP, MQTT, WebSocket) but two small pure helpers that every one of those paths depends on had no inline coverage: `validate_framing(&Cli)` (the single gate that decides whether the CLI combination of `--framing` / `--encoding` can start the server at all) and `build_throttle_config(&Cli)` (the mapping from the `--client-message-rate` / `--client-burst` flag pair to the `Option<ThrottleConfig>` the session loop reads on every message). A regression in either silently corrupts every downstream subsystem the same way, and the framing validator has a delicate feature-gate split (`tak-protobuf` framing and encoding are only valid when `proto` is compiled in) that has historically been easy to break. Ten new tests behind a shared `parse_cli(&[&str])` helper that feeds `Cli::try_parse_from(…)` with the required `--cert/--key/--ca` triplet cover the full observable contract.
